Best Is Not Always Better.
Finetune Handbook

Large context windows can pull in knowledge at inference — but you can't call an API from an embedded device, a compliance boundary, or a 10M-request pipeline. This is where small, specialized models earn their place.

🗺️ The Map→ Foundations→ Data→ Training→ Proving It→ Shipping It │ 🧪 Case Study
Part 0
Orientation
See the whole road before the first line of code
🗺️
Start Here — The Map
One pipeline, eight stages — every chapter in this book is one box on this map

Before the first line of code, it helps to see the whole road. Fine-tuning isn't one technique — it's a pipeline: take raw knowledge, teach a base model your domain, shape it to follow instructions, prove it actually improved, then shrink it down and ship it. Almost every confusing question — "do I need CPT or SFT?", "when do I merge?", "why is my model bigger now?" — dissolves the moment you know which box you're standing in.

How to read this book: the chapters follow this map roughly left-to-right, top-to-bottom. You rarely run all eight stages — a quick task might be just SFT → Evaluate → Deploy; a from-scratch domain model walks the whole path. Two boxes are optional and skipped more often than not: CPT (only when you have a lot of raw domain text) and Align (only when format and taste matter more than facts).

And we won't keep it abstract. We'll build one real example end-to-end — a computational-chemistry model small enough to run on a laptop — and return to it at every stage. So when a later chapter says "remember our chem model," this map is the journey it's talking about.
Part I
Foundations
The libraries every fine-tune is built on
↩ You've seen the whole map. Now meet the four libraries every later chapter leans on — tensors, datasets, models, adapters.
1
PyTorch Core
Tensors · Autograd · Models · Training loop

PyTorch is the foundation everything else is built on. Before loading any model you need to understand how tensors move between devices, how autograd tracks gradients, and how a training loop ties loss, backprop, and optimizer step together. These patterns repeat verbatim inside every Trainer you'll ever use.

🔷 Tensors & Device
import torch

device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"

x = torch.tensor([[1.0, 2.0]])
x = torch.randn(3, 4)            # normal dist
x = x.to(device)                  # move to GPU
x.reshape(2, -1)  x.squeeze()  x.T
torch.cat([a, b], dim=0)          # concat
x.float()  x.long()  x.numpy()
⚡ Autograd
x = torch.randn(3, requires_grad=True)
loss = (x ** 2).sum()
loss.backward()    # compute grads
x.grad             # dy/dx

# Disable for inference
with torch.no_grad():
    pred = model(x)

optimizer.zero_grad()   # 1. clear
loss.backward()          # 2. grads
optimizer.step()         # 3. update
🧠 nn.Module & Layers
import torch.nn as nn

class Net(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self):
        super().__init__()
        self.net = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(784, 256), nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Dropout(0.2),
            nn.Linear(256, 10))
    def forward(self, x):
        return self.net(x)

model = Net().to(device)
# Also: Conv2d, LSTM, Embedding, LayerNorm
🔁 Training Loop + Save
loss_fn = nn.CrossEntropyLoss()
opt = torch.optim.AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)

for epoch in range(epochs):
    model.train()
    for X, y in train_loader:
        X, y = X.to(device), y.to(device)
        loss = loss_fn(model(X), y)
        opt.zero_grad(); loss.backward(); opt.step()
    model.eval()
    with torch.no_grad():
        pass  # validate here

torch.save(model.state_dict(), "model.pt")
model.load_state_dict(torch.load("model.pt"))
⚡ Mixed Precision (AMP) — measured, not assumed
# autocast picks fp16/bf16 per-op; GradScaler keeps grads from underflowing
scaler = torch.amp.GradScaler(device)
for X, y in loader:
    opt.zero_grad()
    with torch.autocast(device_type=device):
        loss = loss_fn(model(X), y)
    scaler.scale(loss).backward()
    scaler.step(opt); scaler.update()
01_pytorch_basics.py §8 times fp32 vs AMP with warmup on a compute-bound stack: ~1.5–2× on Tensor-Core GPUs (measured 1.52× on an RTX 3080 Ti; ~1× on CPU — no Tensor Cores, shown honestly).
⚠️ Always call model.train() before training and model.eval() before validation — Dropout and BatchNorm behave differently in each mode.
Script: 01_pytorch_basics.py — tensors → autograd → nn.Module → training loop → save/load → AMP benchmark, all runnable.
🔧
PyTorch Module Internals
nn.Module · ModuleList gotcha · Weight surgery · HF head swapping

PyTorch's module system is deceptively simple — until you store layers in a plain Python list and nothing trains. This chapter covers the mechanics that senior developers trip over: how nn.Module discovers parameters, why nn.ModuleList exists, how to freeze/unfreeze layers, and what HuggingFace actually does when you call AutoModelForSequenceClassification.

⚠️ The ModuleList Gotcha — #1 Silent Bug
# WRONG — layers are INVISIBLE to PyTorch
class BrokenNet(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self):
        super().__init__()
        self.layers = [nn.Linear(10, 10)
                        for _ in range(3)]
        # ↑ plain list: .parameters() returns NOTHING
        # Won't train, won't save, won't move to GPU

# CORRECT — use nn.ModuleList
class FixedNet(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self):
        super().__init__()
        self.layers = nn.ModuleList([
            nn.Linear(10, 10) for _ in range(3)
        ])
        # ↑ registered! .parameters() finds all 6 tensors

# Same for dicts: use nn.ModuleDict, not {}
self.heads = nn.ModuleDict({
    "cls": nn.Linear(10, 2),
    "reg": nn.Linear(10, 1),
})
Rule: If it has parameters, it must be an nn.Module attribute — not inside a plain Python list or dict.
🔀 Non-Linear Forward Pass
class ResidualBlock(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, dim):
        super().__init__()
        self.ff = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(dim, dim * 4),
            nn.GELU(),
            nn.Linear(dim * 4, dim),
        )
        self.norm = nn.LayerNorm(dim)

    def forward(self, x):
        return self.norm(x + self.ff(x))
        # ↑ skip connection: input goes TWO paths

# Multi-head: shared backbone, different task heads
class MultiTaskModel(nn.Module):
    def forward(self, x, task="cls"):
        features = self.backbone(x)  # shared
        if task == "cls":
            return self.classifier(features)
        return self.regressor(features)
forward() is just Python. Skip connections, branches, conditionals, shared layers — all fine. nn.Sequential is a convenience, not a requirement.
🔬 Weight Surgery — Freeze, Modify, Replace
# Freeze a layer
for p in model.layer1.parameters():
    p.requires_grad = False

# Freeze ALL, then unfreeze just the head
for p in model.parameters():
    p.requires_grad = False
for p in model.head.parameters():
    p.requires_grad = True

# Inspect weights
w = model.layer1.weight         # tensor
print(w.shape, w.data.mean())

# Modify weights directly
with torch.no_grad():
    model.layer1.bias.zero_()
    model.layer1.weight.copy_(other_model.layer1.weight)

# Replace a layer entirely
model.head = nn.Linear(768, 10)  # new, trainable

# Save / load weights
torch.save(model.state_dict(), "w.pt")
model.load_state_dict(torch.load("w.pt"))
🔄 HuggingFace Head Swapping
from transformers import (
    AutoModel,
    AutoModelForCausalLM,
    AutoModelForSequenceClassification,
)

# Same "gpt2" backbone, different heads:
base = AutoModel.from_pretrained("gpt2")
#   → GPT2Model (no head, raw hidden states)

causal = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("gpt2")
#   → GPT2Model + lm_head (768 → 50257)
#     lm_head = pretrained (came with the model)

clf = AutoModelForSequenceClassification.from_pretrained(
    "gpt2", num_labels=3)
#   → GPT2Model + score (768 → 3)
#     score = RANDOMLY INITIALIZED ← train this!
HF strips the original head and adds a task-specific one. The backbone weights are identical. The new head is random — you must fine-tune it.
📋 AutoModelFor* — Which Class Adds Which Head
Class Head Added Output Shape Task
AutoModel None [batch, seq, hidden] Raw features / embeddings
AutoModelForCausalLM lm_head [batch, seq, vocab] Next token prediction
AutoModelForSequenceClassification score / classifier [batch, num_labels] Sentiment, intent, topic
AutoModelForTokenClassification classifier [batch, seq, num_labels] NER, POS tagging
AutoModelForQuestionAnswering qa_outputs [batch, seq, 2] Extractive QA (start/end)
AutoModelForMaskedLM cls [batch, seq, vocab] Fill-in-the-blank (BERT)
AutoModelForSeq2SeqLM lm_head (decoder) [batch, seq, vocab] Translation, summarization
🔍 Inspecting Any Model's Architecture
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("gpt2")

# See the full tree
print(model)

# Top-level children + param counts
for name, child in model.named_children():
    n = sum(p.numel() for p in child.parameters())
    print(f"{name}: {type(child).__name__}  params={n:,}")
# �� transformer: GPT2Model  params=124,439,808
# → lm_head:     Linear     params=38,597,376

# Every parameter name + shape
for name, p in model.named_parameters():
    print(f"{name:40s} {list(p.shape)}")

# Count trainable vs frozen
trainable = sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters() if p.requires_grad)
total = sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters())
print(f"Trainable: {trainable:,} / {total:,}")
Full working example: scripts/13_pytorch_internals.py demonstrates every concept above with live code — the ModuleList bug, weight surgery, head swapping comparison, and model inspection.
2
HF Datasets
Load · Inspect · Map · Filter · Format for training

The Hugging Face Datasets library gives you a single, memory-mapped interface to thousands of public datasets and your own files. Arrow-backed columns mean map/filter transforms run fast even on datasets too large for RAM. Getting data into the right shape — renaming columns, applying chat templates, setting the format to torch — is typically 80% of a fine-tuning project.

📥 Load
from datasets import load_dataset, Dataset

ds = load_dataset("imdb")
ds = load_dataset("nyu-mll/glue", "mrpc", split="train")
ds = load_dataset("json",  data_files="data.jsonl")
ds = load_dataset("csv",   data_files="data.csv")
ds = load_dataset("imdb", streaming=True)  # no download
ds = Dataset.from_dict({
    "text": ["hi", "bye"], "label": [0, 1]})
ds = Dataset.from_pandas(df)
🗺️ map() — Core Transform
def tokenize(examples):
    return tokenizer(examples["text"],
        truncation=True, max_length=128)

# batched=True = 10-100x faster
ds = ds.map(tokenize, batched=True,
             remove_columns=["text"])

# Filter, select, split
ds = ds.filter(lambda x: x["label"] == 1)
ds = ds.select(range(1000))
ds = ds.shuffle(seed=42)
split = ds.train_test_split(test_size=0.2)
🔄 Format for PyTorch
# Set format after all transforms
ds.set_format("torch",
    columns=["input_ids", "attention_mask", "labels"])

from torch.utils.data import DataLoader
loader = DataLoader(ds, batch_size=32, shuffle=True)

# Column ops
ds = ds.rename_column("label", "labels")
ds = ds.remove_columns(["idx"])
ds = ds.select_columns(["input_ids", "labels"])
💾 Save & Push
ds.save_to_disk("my_dataset")      # fast Arrow format
ds = datasets.load_from_disk("my_dataset")
ds.to_json("out.jsonl")
ds.to_csv("out.csv")
ds.push_to_hub("username/my-dataset")

# Speed tip: multiprocessing
ds = ds.map(fn, batched=True, num_proc=4)
⏱️ batched=True — measured, not assumed
# batched=True hands the whole batch to ONE fast-tokenizer call
# instead of N Python round-trips:
ds.map(tok, batched=True, batch_size=1000)
02_hf_datasets.py §11 times it on 4000 rows with a fast (Rust) tokenizer: 8.8× measured on a tiny tokenizer; larger fast tokenizers reach the cheatsheet's 10–100× range. The win comes from amortizing per-call overhead, so it scales with batch size and tokenizer speed.
Script: 02_hf_datasets.py — load → inspect → map → filter → split → format → save → batched benchmark, all runnable.
🗄️
The Dataset Zoo
PyTorch Dataset → DataLoader → HF Dataset → DataCollator — untangled

Four things called "dataset" and nobody explains how they connect. This is the single biggest source of confusion when coming from general Python/ML into the HuggingFace fine-tuning stack. Here's the complete picture — what each layer does, when you need it, and what the Trainer hides from you.

🔶 PyTorch Dataset
from torch.utils.data import Dataset

class MyDataset(Dataset):
    def __init__(self, texts, tokenizer):
        self.texts = texts
        self.tokenizer = tokenizer

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.texts)

    def __getitem__(self, idx):
        return self.tokenizer(self.texts[idx])
What: Abstract base class. You subclass it, implement __len__ + __getitem__.
Storage: Row-oriented, in-memory (everything in RAM).
Use when: Custom data sources, non-text data, legacy code.
For LLM fine-tuning: Almost never needed — HF Dataset replaces this.
🔷 HF datasets.Dataset
from datasets import Dataset, load_dataset

ds = load_dataset("json", data_files="data.jsonl")
ds = Dataset.from_dict({"text": ["hi", "bye"]})

# Transform without subclassing
ds = ds.map(tokenize_fn, batched=True)
ds = ds.filter(lambda x: len(x["text"]) > 10)

# Make it act like a PyTorch Dataset
ds.set_format("torch")
What: Column-oriented, Arrow-backed, memory-mapped from disk.
Storage: Doesn't load everything into RAM. 50GB dataset? No problem.
Has: .map(), .filter(), .select() — no subclassing needed.
Key insight: It implements __getitem__ + __len__, so DataLoader and Trainer accept it directly.
🔀 DataLoader
from torch.utils.data import DataLoader

loader = DataLoader(
    dataset,            # any Dataset (PyTorch or HF)
    batch_size=8,
    shuffle=True,
    num_workers=4,
    collate_fn=collator # ← this is where collators plug in
)

for batch in loader:
    outputs = model(**batch)
What: Wraps a Dataset. Handles batching, shuffling, parallel loading.
collate_fn: Takes a list of individual samples → returns one batch dict.
You rarely create one. Trainer builds it internally from your dataset + collator.
📦 DataCollator
from transformers import (
    DataCollatorWithPadding,          # classification
    DataCollatorForLanguageModeling,  # causal LM / MLM
    DataCollatorForSeq2Seq,           # encoder-decoder
)
from trl import DataCollatorForCompletionOnlyLM # SFT

collator = DataCollatorForLanguageModeling(
    tokenizer=tok, mlm=False)   # causal LM
What: A fancy collate_fn. Takes list of examples → returns padded + labeled batch.
Dynamic padding: Pads to longest in batch, not global max (saves compute).
Creates labels: For causal LM, labels = input_ids with -100 on padding.
Key detail: The label shift for next-token prediction happens inside the model, not in the collator.
⚡ How Trainer Wires It All Together
You provide:                          Trainer creates internally:
─────────────                          ──────────────────────────
train_dataset  (HF Dataset)         →  DataLoader(
  +                                        dataset=train_dataset,
data_collator  (e.g. LM collator)   →      collate_fn=data_collator,
  +                                        batch_size=args.per_device_train_batch_size,
TrainingArguments                    →      sampler=RandomSampler,
                                       )

# The Trainer's default collator selection:
# 1. You provide data_collator           → uses that
# 2. You provide tokenizer, no collator  → DataCollatorWithPadding
# 3. Neither                             → default_data_collator (just stacks)

# SFTTrainer goes even further:
trainer = SFTTrainer(
    model="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B",
    train_dataset=ds,           # just needs a "text" or "messages" column
    args=SFTConfig(output_dir="./out"),
)
# SFTTrainer handles: tokenization → collation → label masking → training
# You never write a collator or DataLoader.
📋 Which Collator For What?
Collator Task What It Does
DataCollatorWithPadding Classification Dynamic padding only. No label manipulation.
DataCollatorForLanguageModeling Causal LM / MLM mlm=False: labels = input_ids, -100 on padding. mlm=True: masks 15% of tokens.
DataCollatorForSeq2Seq Translation, Summarization Pads both input and decoder labels. -100 on label padding.
DataCollatorForCompletionOnlyLM SFT (instruction tuning) Masks prompt tokens with -100. Only completion contributes to loss.
default_data_collator Pre-padded data Just stacks tensors. No padding. Requires uniform sequence lengths.
⚠️ Common Gotchas
1. "I padded in .map() — why do I need a collator?"
You don't need one, but fixed padding wastes compute. A batch of [12, 18, 15]-token sequences pads to 18, not 512. That's the collator's job.

2. "I wrote a PyTorch Dataset subclass"
You almost certainly didn't need to. Dataset.from_dict() or load_dataset() + .map() covers 99% of fine-tuning.

3. "HF Dataset vs PyTorch Dataset — which am I using?"
They're different classes from different libraries. HF datasets.Dataset is NOT a subclass of torch.utils.data.Dataset. It just implements the same interface (duck typing). Both work with DataLoader.

4. "Where does the label shift happen for next-token prediction?"
Not in the collator. The collator sets labels = input_ids. The one-position shift happens inside model.forward(). The model handles its own architecture.

5. "Loss doesn't decrease despite training"
Check labels. If you set labels manually AND use a LM collator, they may conflict. Let the collator handle labels, or don't use one.
Full working example: scripts/12_dataset_ecosystem.py walks through all four layers with live code — PyTorch Dataset, DataLoader, HF Dataset, every collator type, and shows what Trainer builds internally.
3
HF Transformers
Pipeline · Tokenizer · AutoModel · Trainer · Push to Hub

Transformers is the model zoo: tokenizers, AutoModel classes, and the Trainer API in one package. The pipeline abstraction lets you call any model in two lines; AutoTokenizer / AutoModelForCausalLM give you full control. The Trainer handles the training loop, gradient accumulation, mixed precision, and checkpointing automatically.

🚀 Pipeline — Instant Inference
from transformers import pipeline

clf  = pipeline("text-classification")
gen  = pipeline("text-generation",
               model="meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct",
               device_map="auto")
asr  = pipeline("automatic-speech-recognition",
               model="openai/whisper-large-v3")
summ = pipeline("summarization")
ner  = pipeline("token-classification")
qa   = pipeline("question-answering")

gen("Once upon a time", max_new_tokens=50)
✏️ Tokenizer + AutoModel
from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM

tok   = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("bert-base-uncased")
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    "meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct",
    device_map="auto", torch_dtype="auto")

enc = tok("Hello", return_tensors="pt",
          truncation=True, max_length=128).to(model.device)
ids = model.generate(**enc, max_new_tokens=100,
                       temperature=0.7, do_sample=True)
tok.decode(ids[0], skip_special_tokens=True)
🏋️ Trainer — Full Fine-Tuning Loop
from transformers import AutoModelForSequenceClassification, AutoTokenizer, TrainingArguments, Trainer, DataCollatorWithPadding
from datasets import load_dataset

model = AutoModelForSequenceClassification.from_pretrained("distilbert-base-uncased", num_labels=2)
tok   = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("distilbert-base-uncased")
ds    = load_dataset("imdb").map(lambda x: tok(x["text"], truncation=True), batched=True)

args = TrainingArguments(output_dir="./out", num_train_epochs=3, per_device_train_batch_size=16,
    learning_rate=2e-5, eval_strategy="epoch", save_strategy="epoch",
    load_best_model_at_end=True, fp16=True, weight_decay=0.01)

trainer = Trainer(model=model, args=args, train_dataset=ds["train"], eval_dataset=ds["test"],
    processing_class=tok, data_collator=DataCollatorWithPadding(tok))
trainer.train()
trainer.push_to_hub("username/my-model")   # save + push
📊 Evaluation & Metrics — compute_metrics
import numpy as np
# evaluate.load("accuracy") is the library way; inline numpy keeps it offline
def compute_metrics(eval_pred):
    logits, labels = eval_pred
    preds = np.argmax(logits, axis=-1)
    return {"accuracy": float((preds == labels).mean())}

trainer = Trainer(..., compute_metrics=compute_metrics)
trainer.evaluate()                 # {'eval_loss':..., 'eval_accuracy':...}
out = trainer.predict(test_ds)      # out.predictions (logits), out.label_ids
03_hf_transformers.py §5 runs this end-to-end on a tiny set: a real Trainer fit, then evaluate() reports eval_accuracy and predict() returns logits + true labels. Other metrics: swap in "f1", "rouge", "bleu", "seqeval".
Script: 03_hf_transformers.py — tokenizer → AutoModel → pipeline → Trainer → compute_metrics → save/reload, all runnable.
Part II
Preparing Data
You cannot train on data you have not shaped
↩ Tools in hand. Before any training runs, your data has to be in a shape TRL recognizes — that single choice decides CPT vs SFT.
📂
Bring Your Own Data
CSV · JSONL · Plain text → trainer-ready datasets

This is the chapter that quietly decides whether your fine-tune works. Get the data shape right and the trainer does the correct thing automatically; get it subtly wrong and training looks healthy while the model learns the wrong thing. So before any load_dataset() call, we'll build up — from zero — the three ideas everything else rests on: the tokenizer, the chat template, and how those two create the loss mask. Every sequence and number below was produced by running the real SmolLM2-135M-Instruct tokenizer, not sketched.

⚡ Do this first: inspect YOUR data in 10 seconds
Before reading the theory, run this on your own model + one example. It prints the exact string the model will train on and what fraction of tokens are actually graded — the single most useful check in this whole chapter. Paste, change two lines, run: 
# === Paste your model + ONE example. Prints the templated string + the loss mask. ===
from transformers import AutoTokenizer

MODEL      = "HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M-Instruct"     # ← your model
prompt     = [{"role":"user",      "content":"What is HVAC?"}]                       # ← your question
completion = [{"role":"assistant", "content":"Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning."}]  # ← your answer

tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(MODEL)
print("has chat_template:", tok.chat_template is not None)   # None ⇒ base model, no template

# the exact string the model trains on (full turn)
full_text = tok.apply_chat_template(prompt + completion, tokenize=False)
print(full_text)

# reproduce completion_only_loss: mask the prompt span to -100, keep the answer live
p_ids = tok(tok.apply_chat_template(prompt, tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True),
            add_special_tokens=False).input_ids
f_ids = tok(full_text, add_special_tokens=False).input_ids
labels = [-100]*len(p_ids) + f_ids[len(p_ids):]
learned = sum(l != -100 for l in labels)
print(f"masked: {len(p_ids)}  learned: {learned}  unmasked: {learned/len(f_ids):.0%}")
print("LEARNED:", repr(tok.decode([i for i,l in zip(f_ids,labels) if l != -100])))

# ─────────── ACTUAL OUTPUT on SmolLM2-135M-Instruct (run just now) ───────────
# has chat_template: True
# <|im_start|>system … <|im_end|>
# <|im_start|>user\nWhat is HVAC?<|im_end|>
# <|im_start|>assistant\nHeating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning.<|im_end|>
# masked: 34  learned: 12  unmasked: 26%
# LEARNED: 'Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning.<|im_end|>\n'
Read the last two lines. LEARNED should be only your answer (plus the stop token). The unmasked% should land roughly 20–70% for completion-only. 100% = the prompt wasn't masked (you're teaching it to generate questions). 0% = nothing is being learned (template/marker mismatch). If those two lines look right, your data is wired correctly — the rest of this chapter explains why.
① First principles: a model only ever sees a flat list of token IDs
A language model cannot read your Python dict {"role": "user", "content": "What is HVAC?"}. It can't even read text. It reads integers — token IDs. Two objects bridge the gap, and they're worth naming precisely because the rest of the chapter is just consequences of them:
  • The tokenizer turns a string into a list of integer IDs (and back). "What is HVAC?" → [1780, 314, 30551, 47]. That's it — a reversible string↔integer codec the model was trained alongside.
  • The chat template is a small Jinja snippet stored on the tokenizer (tokenizer.chat_template) that turns a list of role/content messages into one flat string, inserting the special control tokens the model was fine-tuned to recognize — like <|im_start|> and <|im_end|>. As the official transformers docs put it: "the list of role and content dictionaries you pass to a chat model gets converted to a token sequence, often with control tokens like <|user|> or <|assistant|>."
Why this matters for data prep: the model has no built-in notion of "user" or "assistant." It only learned that the token <|im_start|>assistant means "your turn starts here." The chat template is the only thing that puts those tokens in the right places. Different model families use different control tokens (ChatML's <|im_start|>, Llama-3's <|start_header_id|>, Mistral's [INST]) — so you never hardcode them; you let the tokenizer's own template do it. Always check what you have first: print(tokenizer.chat_template) — None means a base model with no template (you'll need to supply one).
② See the actual string the model trains on (measured, not sketched)
Take one Q&A pair and run it through apply_chat_template. Here is the exact output from the SmolLM2 tokenizer (ChatML), with the control tokens highlighted: 
msgs = [{"role":"user",      "content":"What is HVAC?"},
        {"role":"assistant", "content":"Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning."}]
text = tok.apply_chat_template(msgs, tokenize=False)   # add_generation_prompt=False (training)

# ── exact output ──
<|im_start|>system
You are a helpful AI assistant named SmolLM, trained by Hugging Face<|im_end|>
<|im_start|>user
What is HVAC?<|im_end|>
<|im_start|>assistant
Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning.<|im_end|>
Three things to notice, because each one bites someone: (1) a system message you never wrote was injected — SmolLM's template adds a default one. (2) Every turn is wrapped in <|im_start|>role … <|im_end|>. (3) The whole conversation became one string — the dict structure is gone. That string is what gets tokenized into IDs and fed to the model.
The duplicate-special-tokens trap: the template already inserts all needed special tokens. If you render with tokenize=False and then tokenize the string yourself, pass add_special_tokens=False — otherwise the tokenizer prepends another <bos> and you train on a malformed sequence. (Using tokenize=True directly avoids this entirely — the safer option, per the transformers docs.)
③ How that string becomes the loss mask — the whole game, token by token
SFT for a Q&A model should teach it to produce the answer, not to recite the question. "Completion-only loss" does exactly that by setting every prompt token's label to -100 (PyTorch's cross-entropy ignore index) and keeping only the answer tokens live. Here is the real 46-token sequence from the pair above, with the actual mask TRL computes — 34 masked, 12 learned: 
# idx  token                     label
[ 0] '<|im_start|>'            -100   🚫 system…
 ...  (system + user turn)      -100   🚫
[26] ' HVAC'                   -100   🚫
[27] '?'                       -100   🚫
[28] '<|im_end|>'              -100   🚫
[30] '<|im_start|>'            -100   🚫
[31] 'ass' [32] 'istant' [33] '\n'  -100   🚫  ← prompt ends here
[34] 'He'                      3681   ✏️ learn  ← answer starts
[35] 'ating' ... [43] '.'              ✏️ learn
[44] '<|im_end|>'                    ✏️ learn  ← learn to STOP, too
[45] '\n'                            ✏️ learn
# measured: 34/46 masked, 12 learned → 26% unmasked
Read what the model is being graded on: only positions 34–45 — the answer text plus the closing <|im_end|> (so it learns where to stop). The attention is never masked — the model still reads the whole question — we simply refuse to grade its predictions on the prompt. Mask the wrong span and the loss still drops; you've just taught the wrong skill.
The boundary the mask keys off is literally <|im_start|>assistant\n — the template's own control tokens. That's why "data prep" and "loss" are the same topic: the chat template draws the line, and the line is where the mask flips from 🚫 to ✏️. Full mechanics + the three ways to set this: Loss-Masking chapter.
④ One flag flips the same template between training and inference
The same apply_chat_template is used at train time and at generation time — the only difference is add_generation_prompt, and getting it backwards is a top "why is my model broken" cause:
TrainingInference
flagadd_generation_prompt=False (default)add_generation_prompt=True
you passthe full conversation (incl. the assistant answer)only the user turn(s)
string ends with…Air Conditioning.<|im_end|>…<|im_start|>assistant\n (open turn)
so the model…learns "given the full turn, predict each next token"is asked to write the assistant turn from here
Per the transformers docs: "Set add_generation_prompt=False [during training] because the additional tokens to prompt an assistant response aren't helpful during training." At inference you flip it to True so the string stops exactly at <|im_start|>assistant\n and the model continues from there. Train with the full turn; generate with the open turn. Same template, one flag.
⚠️ The tripwire: who applies the template — and why add_generation_prompt is asymmetric
Two questions cause more silent SFT failures than anything else, and the honest answers are not symmetric. Both are verified against the installed TRL source (sft_trainer.py) and the transformers docs, not guessed.
Q1 — Do I apply the chat template, or does the trainer?
For training: the trainer does it — when (and only when) your data is conversational. If your rows are messages, or prompt/completion as message lists, SFTTrainer calls apply_chat_template internally. Do not pre-apply it yourself — you'd double-wrap the special tokens. If your rows are plain {"text": …} strings, the trainer does not template (there are no roles to template) — what you put in the string is what it trains on.
For inference: you do it. The trainer isn't involved at generation time — you call apply_chat_template(...) in your own code.
Q2 — Where does add_generation_prompt go? (the asymmetry)
It is an inference-time flag you set to True so the string ends at the open assistant turn (…<|im_start|>assistant\n) and the model continues. At training time the full sequence must contain the real answer, so the conversation is not left open. You essentially never pass add_generation_prompt=True yourself for training.
The subtlety that surprises people (and looks like a contradiction): for a prompt-completion dataset, TRL internally renders the prompt half with add_generation_prompt=True — but only to find where the prompt ends so it can mask it. It then renders prompt + completion normally for the actual training tokens. So "training uses False" is the rule you act on; under the hood TRL uses True on the prompt purely as a measuring tape for the loss mask. You never set this — the trainer does, correctly. (Verified: sft_trainer.py tokenize step, prompt-completion branch.)
Your data Template applied by… add_generation_prompt You must…
TRAIN · messages / conversational prompt+completionthe trainerFalse (TRL handles the prompt-mask internally)pass raw messages — don't pre-template
TRAIN · plain {"text"}you, before training (or nobody, for CPT)False when you render itrender with tokenize=False, add_special_tokens=False
INFERENCE · any chat modelyou, in your generate codeTruestop at the open assistant turn, then generate()
The failure this prevents: if you pre-template a conversational dataset into text and let the trainer template again → doubled <|im_start|>/<bos> tokens, and training silently learns a malformed format. At inference, forgetting add_generation_prompt=True → the model "continues the user's message instead of replying to it" (transformers docs). Two different mistakes, same symptom: "my fine-tune is broken." Sources: transformers · Model training · TRL SFT Trainer · installed trl/trainer/sft_trainer.py.
Now the mechanics: loading & reshaping your files
📥 Loading Your Files
from datasets import load_dataset

# CSV
ds = load_dataset("csv", data_files="my_data.csv")

# JSONL (one JSON object per line)
ds = load_dataset("json", data_files="my_data.jsonl")

# Plain text (one sample per line)
ds = load_dataset("text", data_files="corpus.txt")

# Multiple files / directory
ds = load_dataset("csv", data_files="data/*.csv")
🔄 Column Mapping
# Your CSV has "question" + "answer" columns
# SFTTrainer needs "prompt" + "completion"

ds = ds.map(lambda x: {
    "prompt": x["question"],
    "completion": x["answer"],
}, remove_columns=["question", "answer"])

# Or just rename if columns already match
ds = ds.rename_columns({
    "instruction": "prompt",
    "output": "completion",
})
Format Cheatsheet — What Each Trainer Expects
Trainer Required Columns Example
Causal LM "text" {"text": "LoRA injects low-rank matrices..."}
SFT "prompt" + "completion"
or "messages"		 {"prompt": "What is LoRA?", "completion": "LoRA adds..."}
DPO / ORPO "prompt" + "chosen" + "rejected" {"prompt": "...", "chosen": "good", "rejected": "bad"}
GRPO "prompt" {"prompt": "What is 2+2?"} (reward from functions)
🧹 Data Hygiene
# Filter short/empty rows
ds = ds.filter(lambda x: len(x["text"]) > 10)

# Remove duplicates
seen = set()
def dedup(x):
    if x["text"] in seen: return False
    seen.add(x["text"]); return True
ds = ds.filter(dedup)

# Train/test split
splits = ds.train_test_split(test_size=0.2, seed=42)

# Save for reuse
ds.save_to_disk("processed_dataset/")
Always split before training. Without an eval set, you can't detect overfitting.
Full working example: scripts/11_custom_data.py demonstrates loading CSV, JSONL, and text files, converting to every trainer format, filtering, deduplication, and saving — all runnable with no GPU.
🧩 "Should I apply the chat template myself, or keep prompt + completion?" — the confusion, settled
You'll see tutorials that take {"prompt", "completion"}, run apply_chat_template over it, and store the result in a single "text" column before training. It works — but it silently changes what loss you're computing, and that's the source of the confusion. Here's the rule, verified against the installed TRL source:
The mask lives in the columns, not the text. TRL builds a completion_mask only when it can see separate prompt and completion fields. Once you flatten everything into one "text" string, that boundary is gone — there is nothing left to mask, so the only option is full-sequence loss.
What you hand TRL Who applies the template Loss you get completion_only_loss?
prompt + completion (strings or message lists) TRL does it (for conversational) and keeps the boundary Completion-only (prompt → -100) ✅ available; True by default
messages (multi-turn) TRL does it Full, or assistant-only if you opt in use assistant_only_loss=True
You pre-rendered into "text" (template already applied) You did, before TRL saw it Full sequence (every token graded) ❌ not available — see below
formatting_func returning a string Your function Full sequence ❌ converts to a language-modeling dataset
This isn't a style preference — TRL enforces it. On the installed version, asking for completion-only loss on a flattened/text dataset raises: "The completion_only_loss argument is not supported for language modeling datasets." And the docs state a formatting_func "explicitly converts the dataset into a language modeling type." So: flatten-to-text and completion-only loss are mutually exclusive.
So when is pre-flattening the right move? Three legitimate cases:
  • You actually want full-sequence loss — i.e. CPT / continued pretraining on chat-formatted text (you want it to learn the whole turn structure).
  • Your data doesn't fit the four shapes (odd columns like Alpaca's instruction/input/output) and you'd rather format once with formatting_func — accepting full-sequence loss.
  • You're using Unsloth's train_on_responses_only — it re-introduces the mask after templating (illustrated just below), the supported way to get completion-only behavior from already-templated chat text.
Default recommendation: for instruction/Q&A SFT, keep your data as prompt + completion (or messages) and let TRL apply the template. You get correct completion-only masking for free, and your dataset stays inspectable. Only flatten to "text" when you genuinely want full-sequence loss. Full mechanics in the Loss-Masking chapter; sources: TRL SFT Trainer · Dataset formats.
🔁 What train_on_responses_only actually does — illustrated on the real sequence
Here's the case the clarity card calls the exception. On the Unsloth path you often hand the trainer already-templated chat text (one flat string per row), so TRL has no prompt/completion boundary to build a mask from — by default it would grade every token (full-sequence). train_on_responses_only recreates completion-only masking after templating, by string-matching the role markers: it finds each response_part marker, keeps the tokens after it live, and sets everything else (system + user spans) to -100. Per Unsloth's docs it "masks the user question and trains the model to output the assistant response with higher weighting."

You give it the two markers that bracket the turns (these must match your template's tokens byte-for-byte — ChatML shown; Llama-3 differs):

from unsloth.chat_templates import train_on_responses_only   # UNSLOTH, not TRL
trainer = train_on_responses_only(
    trainer,
    instruction_part = "<|im_start|>user\n",        # everything from here … 
    response_part    = "<|im_start|>assistant\n",   # … up to here becomes -100
)

The effect, on the same conversation — the markers split the flat string into masked vs learned spans:

# the one flat templated string, color-coded by resulting label:
<|im_start|>user                            🚫 -100  (instruction_part marker)
What is HVAC?<|im_end|>                   🚫 -100  (the question — masked)
<|im_start|>assistant                       🚫 -100  (response_part marker itself)
Heating, Ventilation, and Air Cond…    ✏️ learn (the answer — graded)
<|im_end|>                              ✏️ learn (learn to stop)
# identical result to completion_only_loss=True — same 12 tokens learned —
# but reconstructed by MARKER MATCHING instead of from prompt/completion columns.
Why two ways exist, and which to pick. completion_only_loss=True (TRL) needs prompt+completion columns and gets the boundary for free from your data. train_on_responses_only (Unsloth) works on flattened chat text by matching markers — more flexible (handles multi-turn, custom formats), but it silently masks everything if your marker strings don't match the template exactly — the classic "all labels are -100, loss looks fine, model learns nothing." Its upside: you can inspect trainer.train_dataset[0]["labels"] and literally see the mask. Either way, run the one-line health-check before training.
Sources: Unsloth FAQs · marker-mismatch issue #1290. Verified signature (installed unsloth_zoo): train_on_responses_only(trainer, instruction_part=None, response_part=None, force_match=True, …).
🧭 Recipe cards — pick the data shape + loss for your situation
The interplay in one place: your situation → the data shape you build → the loss that follows. These are the five that cover almost everything.
A · Single-turn Q&A on an instruct model
Build {"prompt","completion"} (strings). Leave completion_only_loss at its default — TRL masks the prompt automatically. The 80%-case for "teach my model to answer questions in my domain." LoRA LR ≈ 1e-4.
B · Multi-turn chat (user↔assistant↔…)
Build {"messages":[…]} and set assistant_only_loss=True so every user turn in the middle is masked, every assistant turn is learned. Requires a template with {% generation %} markers (TRL auto-patches Qwen3/Llama-3/Gemma/Phi-3/…; not SmolLM2).
C · Turn a base model into an instruct model
Base model + a chat dataset. Pass SFTConfig(chat_template_path="…") to import a template, add its special tokens, and resize embeddings — then it trains like B. Needs thousands of examples; a few hundred won't instill chat behavior.
D · CPT / domain adaptation on raw text
Build {"text": …} (raw documents). completion_only_loss=False — you want loss on every token. Turn packing=True for efficiency. This teaches knowledge/style, not answering — usually followed by an SFT pass (A/B).
★ E · New domain + only a little SFT data (the realistic hard case)
Domain the model doesn't know, and you have just a few hundred Q&A pairs. Start from the instruct model, not the base — your SFT set is too small to teach "be an assistant" from scratch, but it's enough to nudge domain style on top of existing chat behavior. Sequence: optional light CPT on domain text (Recipe D, 1 epoch, replay 5–10% general chat to fight forgetting) → small SFT on prompt/completion (Recipe A). Smoke-test chat ability between the two stages; if CPT scrambled it, lower the LR/epochs before SFT. This is exactly the path the 🧪 Case Study walks, and the data-scarcity reason it matters is measured there (~99× raw-vs-instruction gap).
Recipe E's "instruct over base for small SFT" guidance follows current catastrophic-forgetting research (replay is the most effective single mitigation): Domain-Adaptive CPT of Small LMs (arXiv 2504.09687) · Replay to Remember (arXiv 2504.17780). Base-model CPT can win with very large corpora + merging, but that's a different regime.
🧫
Synthetic Data Generation
Beat the ~99× data-scarcity gap without hand-writing thousands of pairs

Raw text is free; instruction pairs are not (the case study hit a ~99× gap). Synthesis closes it — an LLM writes training data for you — but the quality control is the real work. Reference: Data Gen · Merge · HF cheatsheet. 🧪 Runnable: scripts/19_synth_data.py distills pairs from a local Ollama teacher (the chem-tuned SmolLM3 we deployed) and runs this exact QC — tested end-to-end, generating real domain Q&A and dropping leaks/dupes/short ones.

The uncomfortable arithmetic: raw domain text is everywhere, labelled instruction pairs are nowhere — in the case study the gap was ~99×. You can hand-write a few hundred pairs; you can't hand-write ten thousand. Synthetic data is the escape hatch — you let a stronger model write the examples — and the trap is thinking the generation is the hard part. It isn't; calling an LLM in a loop is trivial. The hard part, the part that decides whether your fine-tune learns or rots, is the quality control after.

The cards run cheapest-to-most-careful: distillation (a strong teacher answers questions mined from your own corpus), self-instruct (grow a tiny seed set into a diverse one), then the non-negotiable QC stage — because synthetic data faithfully inherits the teacher's mistakes and mannerisms. The number worth memorizing: 200 clean pairs beat 5,000 noisy ones for a small model.
🗺️ The methods
MethodIdea
Distillationstrong teacher answers your prompts
Self-Instructseeds → LLM writes new tasks+answers
Evol-InstructLLM rewrites prompts harder/deeper
Magpieprompt-only: aligned LLM emits its own queries
Best for clear, checkable tasks. Pair synthesis with a few human-written domain seeds.
🧬 Distillation (the quick win)
def make_pair(q):
    r = client.chat.completions.create(
        model="strong-model", temperature=0.4,
        messages=[{"role":"user","content":q}])
    return {"prompt":q,
            "completion":r.choices[0].message.content}
Generate questions from your corpus headings, keep a strong model's answers as gold. Cheapest bootstrap for an SFT set.
♻️ Self-Instruct loop
seed = load_seed_tasks()          # 10–50 humans
for _ in range(rounds):
    few = random.sample(seed, 6)
    new = llm(f"Tasks:\n{few}\nWrite 5 NEW diverse ones")
    seed += dedupe(parse(new))   # embedding/rouge dedupe
Without aggressive dedupe you get thousands of near-clones. Diversity is the whole game.
⚠️ Quality control (non-negotiable)
Decontaminate against your eval set (or you measure leakage). Dedupe (exact + near). Filter (length, language, refusals, format). Judge a sample for correctness.
200 clean, verified pairs beat 5,000 noisy ones for a small model. Synthetic data inherits the teacher's errors and style.
Part III
Training — Teaching the Model
CPT teaches the domain, SFT teaches the format, alignment teaches the taste
↩ Data ready. Now the actual teaching — and the loss-masking that quietly decides whether it worked.
4
LoRA & QLoRA Concepts
Parameter-efficient fine-tuning — the theory you need

LoRA (Hu et al., 2021) freezes the original weight matrix W and adds a low-rank bypass ΔW = BA (rank r ≪ d). Only A and B are trained — typically 0.1–1% of total parameters — cutting VRAM by 60-80%. QLoRA (Dettmers et al., 2023) goes further: quantise the frozen base to 4-bit NF4, then apply LoRA on top. These two papers made 70B fine-tuning on a single consumer GPU practical.

The big matrix W never moves — so it can be quantized to 4-bit (that's the "Q" in QLoRA) and shared across every adapter. Only the slim A and B learn. At deploy time you fold B·A back into W (see Model Merging) and the detour vanishes.
🧩 What is LoRA?
Freeze the base model. Inject small rank-r matrices (A and B) into attention layers.

Original weight W stays frozen.
Update: ΔW = A × B (r ≪ d)

Only A and B are trained → <1% of total params.

r (rank) = adapter size. Higher r = more expressiveness, more VRAM.
lora_alpha = scaling factor. Keep = r for simplicity.
rsLoRA (use_rslora=True) scales alpha by √r instead of r — better gradient flow at high ranks.
🔢 QLoRA = LoRA + 4-bit
Base model quantized to 4-bit NF4 (NormalFloat4).
LoRA adapters trained in bf16.

Result:
• 8B model: ~16GB → ~5.4GB
• Enables fine-tuning on single consumer GPU

Trade-off: ~39% slower than full LoRA.

When done, merge adapters back into base for inference — no overhead at runtime.
4-bit training = QLoRA. load_in_4bit=True in from_pretrained.
📊 Pick Your Config
ParamValueEffect
r8–16Standard, balanced
r32–64More expressive, more VRAM
lora_alpha= rNeutral scaling
lora_alpha2× rStronger updates
dropout0Recommended by Unsloth
target_modulesall projectionsBest quality
Target all: q_proj k_proj v_proj o_proj gate_proj up_proj down_proj
🧮 LoRA vs QLoRA vs Full FT — and how to prove it
MethodVRAM (8B)Throughput*When to use
Full FT80GB+Highest per-step*Multi-GPU (FSDP) — won't fit one card
LoRA~16–24GB≈ Full FT (±adapter)1× A100 / A6000, great quality
QLoRA~5.5GB~39% slower than LoRASingle consumer GPU / Colab
*A word on "speed": throughput is only comparable where a method actually fits. Full FT has the highest per-step throughput when memory isn't the bottleneck (no adapter matmuls, no 4-bit dequant) — but an 8B full fine-tune needs ~80–100 GB, so on one GPU it doesn't run at all, and sharded across many GPUs the communication overhead often makes wall-clock slower than a single-GPU LoRA run. On the same GPU where both fit, LoRA lands roughly level with Full FT — so the real win of LoRA/QLoRA isn't raw speed, it's memory and accessibility.

These aren't magic numbers. 15_lora_footprint.py counts the trainable parameters straight from Llama-3.1-8B's layer dimensions — no GPU or download needed — and reproduces the headline figure exactly: 

 rank r |    trainable | % of model
--------+--------------+-----------
      8 |       21.0M  |      0.26%
     16 |       41.9M  |      0.52%   <- the "0.52%" claim, demonstrated
     32 |       83.9M  |      1.04%
     64 |      167.8M  |      2.09%
It also does a live PEFT attach (verifying the A×B mechanism on a real module) and reports actual VRAM via torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated() when a GPU is present.
Script: 15_lora_footprint.py — every number in the tables above, runnable.  ·  LoRA paper  ·  QLoRA paper
⚙️
The Engines — Unsloth vs TRL / Transformers
Same fine-tune, different machinery — when to reach for which

TRL+Transformers is the flexible standard; Unsloth is the fast single-GPU specialist with hand-written kernels. They produce the same kind of adapter — the differences are speed, memory, model coverage, and a couple of sharp edges. References: Unsloth cheatsheet · SFT Trainer cheatsheet. 🧪 Runnable: scripts/03_cpt.py runs the same CPT both ways (--backend hf / --backend unsloth); scripts/08_unsloth_vs_hf.py benchmarks them head-to-head.

📄 Full runnable code — case_study/scripts/08_unsloth_vs_hf.py · 186 lines (click to expand)
# verbatim from case_study/scripts/08_unsloth_vs_hf.py @ 4e1e381 — re-sync if the script changes · run with the repo .venv
r"""
08_unsloth_vs_hf.py — head-to-head: the SAME SFT workload via HF/PEFT vs Unsloth.

This answers research question #5 (workflow differences) with measured numbers: wall-clock time,
peak VRAM, and trainable-parameter count, for an identical small SFT run. To keep it a clean
speed/VRAM comparison we train plain language-modeling on a "Q: .. / A: .." text field (loss
semantics are covered in §3/§5), with a FIXED step count so both backends do the same work.

THE ENV SPLIT (the workflow difference itself): Unsloth pins trl/xformers, so it lives in `.venv`;
the HF/PEFT path lives in `.venv-rl`. A single process can't import both. So this script measures the
backend available in the current interpreter, writes its numbers, then shells out to the OTHER venv's
python to measure the other, and finally prints the comparison.

CONTRACT: TRIAL/FULL, idempotent (caches outputs/bench_<backend>_<mode>.json), reproducible.

Run:  python case_study/08_unsloth_vs_hf.py            # measures HF here, shells to .venv for Unsloth
      ./.venv/bin/python case_study/08_unsloth_vs_hf.py --only unsloth   # (what the shell-out runs)
"""
from __future__ import annotations
import argparse
import importlib.util
import json
import subprocess
import sys
import time
from pathlib import Path

import torch

sys.path.insert(0, str(Path(__file__).resolve().parent))
import config  # noqa: E402

ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent              # case_study/scripts/
REPO = ROOT.parent.parent                            # repo root (holds .venv / .venv-rl)


def _sib(fname: str, name: str):
    spec = importlib.util.spec_from_file_location(name, ROOT / fname)
    m = importlib.util.module_from_spec(spec); spec.loader.exec_module(m)
    return m


def _text_dataset():
    rows = _sib("05_sft.py", "s5_for_08").load_seed(None)
    from datasets import Dataset
    return Dataset.from_list([{"text": f"Q: {r['prompt']}\nA: {r['completion']}"} for r in rows])


def _bench_path(backend: str) -> Path:
    return config.OUTPUTS / f"bench_{backend}_{config.RUN_MODE}.json"


def measure_hf() -> dict:
    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer
    from trl import SFTConfig, SFTTrainer
    from peft import LoraConfig
    lim = config.limits()
    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(config.INSTRUCT_MODEL)
    if tok.pad_token is None:
        tok.pad_token = tok.eos_token
    if torch.cuda.is_available():
        torch.cuda.reset_peak_memory_stats()
    args = SFTConfig(output_dir="/tmp/bench_hf", per_device_train_batch_size=config.SFT["batch_size"],
                     gradient_accumulation_steps=1, max_steps=max(lim["sft_max_steps"], 10),
                     learning_rate=config.SFT["lr"], max_length=config.SFT["max_seq_len"],
                     dataset_text_field="text", packing=False, bf16=torch.cuda.is_available(),
                     logging_steps=1000, save_strategy="no", report_to=[], seed=config.SEED)
    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
        config.INSTRUCT_MODEL, dtype=torch.bfloat16 if torch.cuda.is_available() else torch.float32)
    trainer = SFTTrainer(model=model, args=args, train_dataset=_text_dataset(), processing_class=tok,
                         peft_config=LoraConfig(r=config.SFT["lora_r"], lora_alpha=config.SFT["lora_alpha"],
                                                use_rslora=config.SFT["use_rslora"],
                                                target_modules=config.SFT["target_modules"], task_type="CAUSAL_LM"))
    trainable = sum(p.numel() for p in trainer.model.parameters() if p.requires_grad)
    t0 = time.perf_counter(); trainer.train(); dt = time.perf_counter() - t0
    vram = torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated() / 1e9 if torch.cuda.is_available() else 0.0
    return dict(backend="hf", seconds=dt, peak_vram_gb=vram, trainable_params=trainable,
                steps=args.max_steps, env=config.record_env())


def measure_unsloth() -> dict:
    from unsloth import FastLanguageModel, is_bfloat16_supported
    from trl import SFTConfig, SFTTrainer
    lim = config.limits()
    if torch.cuda.is_available():
        torch.cuda.reset_peak_memory_stats()
    model, tok = FastLanguageModel.from_pretrained(
        model_name=config.INSTRUCT_MODEL, max_seq_length=config.SFT["max_seq_len"],
        dtype=None, load_in_4bit=False)
    model = FastLanguageModel.get_peft_model(
        model, r=config.SFT["lora_r"], lora_alpha=config.SFT["lora_alpha"],
        target_modules=config.SFT["target_modules"], use_rslora=config.SFT["use_rslora"],
        use_gradient_checkpointing="unsloth", random_state=config.SEED)
    trainable = sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters() if p.requires_grad)
    args = SFTConfig(output_dir="/tmp/bench_unsloth", per_device_train_batch_size=config.SFT["batch_size"],
                     gradient_accumulation_steps=1, max_steps=max(lim["sft_max_steps"], 10),
                     learning_rate=config.SFT["lr"], max_seq_length=config.SFT["max_seq_len"],
                     dataset_text_field="text", packing=False, bf16=is_bfloat16_supported(),
                     logging_steps=1000, save_strategy="no", report_to=[], seed=config.SEED)
    trainer = SFTTrainer(model=model, tokenizer=tok, train_dataset=_text_dataset(), args=args)
    t0 = time.perf_counter(); trainer.train(); dt = time.perf_counter() - t0
    vram = torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated() / 1e9 if torch.cuda.is_available() else 0.0
    return dict(backend="unsloth", seconds=dt, peak_vram_gb=vram, trainable_params=trainable,
                steps=args.max_steps, env=config.record_env())


def _current_backend() -> str:
    return "unsloth" if importlib.util.find_spec("unsloth") else "hf"


def _other_python(cur: str) -> Path:
    return REPO / (".venv/bin/python" if cur == "hf" else ".venv-rl/bin/python")


def run(force: bool = False) -> dict:
    config.set_all_seeds()
    cur = _current_backend()
    print(f"=== §8 Unsloth-vs-HF | mode={config.RUN_MODE} | this interpreter = {cur} ===")

    # measure the backend available here
    p = _bench_path(cur)
    if p.exists() and not force:
        print(f"  [cached] {cur}: {json.loads(p.read_text())['seconds']:.2f}s")
    else:
        m = (measure_unsloth if cur == "unsloth" else measure_hf)()
        p.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True); p.write_text(json.dumps(m, indent=2))
        print(f"  measured {cur}: {m['seconds']:.2f}s | peak VRAM {m['peak_vram_gb']:.2f} GB | "
              f"trainable {m['trainable_params']:,}")

    # shell out to the other env (only at top level, not recursively)
    other = "hf" if cur == "unsloth" else "unsloth"
    op = _bench_path(other)
    other_py = _other_python(cur)
    if not op.exists() or force:
        if other_py.exists():
            print(f"  shelling out to {other_py.name}-env to measure {other}...")
            subprocess.run([str(other_py), str(ROOT / "08_unsloth_vs_hf.py"), "--only", other,
                            "--mode", config.RUN_MODE] + (["--force"] if force else []),
                           cwd=str(ROOT))
        else:
            print(f"  [skip] {other} env not found at {other_py} — run it there manually.")

    _compare()
    return {"compared": [cur, other]}


def _compare() -> None:
    hf, us = _bench_path("hf"), _bench_path("unsloth")
    if not (hf.exists() and us.exists()):
        print("  (run in both envs to see the comparison table)")
        return
    a, b = json.loads(hf.read_text()), json.loads(us.read_text())
    print("\n=== HF vs Unsloth (same SFT workload, measured on this machine) ===")
    print(f"  {'metric':18} | {'HF/PEFT':>12} | {'Unsloth':>12}")
    print(f"  {'-'*18}-+-{'-'*12}-+-{'-'*12}")
    print(f"  {'wall time (s)':18} | {a['seconds']:>12.2f} | {b['seconds']:>12.2f}")
    print(f"  {'peak VRAM (GB)':18} | {a['peak_vram_gb']:>12.2f} | {b['peak_vram_gb']:>12.2f}")
    print(f"  {'trainable params':18} | {a['trainable_params']:>12,} | {b['trainable_params']:>12,}")
    if b["seconds"]:
        print(f"\n  Unsloth speedup vs HF: {a['seconds']/b['seconds']:.2f}x (this run, this GPU)")
    print("  Note: trainable-param differences reflect each backend's LoRA/embedding choices (§3 §9).")


def main() -> None:
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="HF vs Unsloth speed/VRAM head-to-head.")
    ap.add_argument("--only", choices=["hf", "unsloth"], help="measure just one backend (no shell-out)")
    ap.add_argument("--force", action="store_true")
    ap.add_argument("--mode", choices=["trial", "full"])
    args = ap.parse_args()
    if args.mode:
        config.set_mode(args.mode)
    if args.only:
        fn = measure_unsloth if args.only == "unsloth" else measure_hf
        p = _bench_path(args.only)
        if p.exists() and not args.force:
            print(f"[cached] {args.only}")
            return
        m = fn(); p.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True); p.write_text(json.dumps(m, indent=2))
        print(f"measured {args.only}: {m['seconds']:.2f}s | VRAM {m['peak_vram_gb']:.2f} GB")
    else:
        run(force=args.force)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
Both roads end at the same place — a LoRA adapter you can merge and ship — so the choice isn't about the result, it's about the journey. TRL + Transformers is the standard-issue toolkit: every architecture, multi-GPU, every method (DPO/ORPO/GRPO), at the cost of leaving performance on the table. Unsloth is the tuned race car: hand-written CUDA kernels make the same fine-tune run ~2× faster in ~60% less memory — but on a single GPU, and only on architectures they've patched.

So the decision is short: one consumer GPU and a supported model → take the race car; many GPUs, an exotic architecture, or preference tuning → take the toolkit. The catch is the race car's sharp edges — and the gotchas card exists because every bullet in it is a debugging session someone already paid for.
⚖️ Side by side
UnslothHF + TRL
speed / VRAM~2× / −60%baseline
GPUssingle onlymulti-GPU / FSDP
model coveragepatched subseteverything
4-bit SFTrock-solidcan mis-cast
GGUF exportone callvia llama.cpp
🧭 When to use which
Unsloth: single consumer GPU / Colab, QLoRA on a supported model, you want speed + easy GGUF.
TRL + Transformers: multi-GPU, an unsupported architecture, DPO/ORPO/GRPO, or maximum flexibility.
Both: use the same SFTConfig/dataset — switching is mostly the model-loading line.
🪤 The gotchas that cost hours
• "expected BFloat16 but found Float" on 4-bit SFT with plain TRL — it re-prepares the quantized model and upcasts lm_head. → route QLoRA SFT through Unsloth FastLanguageModel.
• Import order: import unsloth first (before transformers/trl) or its patches don't apply.
• Pickling crash: set dataset_num_proc=1 with Unsloth.
• Version pins: Unsloth historically pins trl<0.9/xformers — keep it in its own venv, separate from a trl-1.x install.
5
Causal LM Fine-Tuning & Continued Pretraining
Raw text training · next-token prediction · domain adaptation

Causal language modelling trains the model to predict the next token from raw text — no labels needed. It's the right approach when you want to adapt the model's knowledge or writing style rather than teach it a specific instruction format. Continued pretraining on domain text (medical, legal, code) shifts the model's priors before you apply SFT on top. DataCollatorForLanguageModeling(mlm=False) auto-constructs the shifted labels from your input tokens.

This is the recipe the 🧪 Case Study follows end to end. The masks differ on purpose: CPT grades everything so the model absorbs the domain; SFT grades only the completion so it learns to answer rather than to echo. Getting that second mask wrong is the single most common reason "SFT came out worse than CPT."
📖 Causal LM Fine-Tuning — What & When
SFT (instruction) = model learns Q→A pairs with chat template.
Causal LM FT = model learns to continue raw text — next-token prediction on your corpus.

Use causal LM fine-tuning when:
  • You want domain-style text generation (legal, medical, code)
  • Your data is raw documents, not instruction pairs
  • You're doing continued pretraining on a new corpus
  • Building a base model before instruction tuning
Key: labels = input_ids — the model predicts every next token. Loss is computed on all tokens (unlike SFT where you can mask the prompt).
🔁 Causal LM Fine-Tune — Full Example
from transformers import (AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer,
    Trainer, TrainingArguments, DataCollatorForLanguageModeling)
from datasets import load_dataset

model_name = "meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B"   # base, not instruct
tok   = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)
tok.pad_token = tok.eos_token              # required for causal LM
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    model_name, device_map="auto", torch_dtype="auto")

# Load raw text corpus
ds = load_dataset("text", data_files={"train":"corpus.txt"})
# or from Hub: load_dataset("wikitext","wikitext-103-raw-v1")

def tokenize(batch):
    enc = tok(batch["text"], truncation=True,
               max_length=2048, padding="max_length")
    enc["labels"] = enc["input_ids"].copy()  # ← predict every token
    return enc

ds = ds.map(tokenize, batched=True, remove_columns=["text"])

# DataCollator handles labels automatically
collator = DataCollatorForLanguageModeling(
    tokenizer=tok, mlm=False)  # mlm=False → causal (GPT-style)

trainer = Trainer(
    model=model,
    args=TrainingArguments(
        output_dir="./causal-ft",
        num_train_epochs=1,
        per_device_train_batch_size=4,
        gradient_accumulation_steps=8,   # eff. batch = 32
        learning_rate=2e-5,
        lr_scheduler_type="cosine",
        warmup_ratio=0.05,
        fp16=True,
        logging_steps=50,
        save_strategy="epoch",
    ),
    train_dataset=ds["train"],
    data_collator=collator,
)
trainer.train()
model.save_pretrained("./causal-ft")
🌱 Continued Pretraining with Unsloth
from unsloth import FastLanguageModel
from trl import SFTTrainer
from transformers import TrainingArguments
from datasets import load_dataset

model, tokenizer = FastLanguageModel.from_pretrained(
    "unsloth/Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-bnb-4bit",
    max_seq_length=2048, load_in_4bit=True)

model = FastLanguageModel.get_peft_model(model,
    r=16, lora_alpha=16, lora_dropout=0,
    target_modules=["q_proj","k_proj","v_proj","o_proj",
                    "gate_proj","up_proj","down_proj"],
    use_rslora=True, use_gradient_checkpointing="unsloth")

# Raw text dataset — no chat template
ds = load_dataset("text", data_files="domain_corpus.txt",
                   split="train")

trainer = SFTTrainer(
    model=model, tokenizer=tokenizer,
    train_dataset=ds,
    dataset_text_field="text",   # raw text column
    max_seq_length=2048,
    packing=True,               # pack documents end-to-end
    args=TrainingArguments(
        output_dir="./pretrain-out",
        num_train_epochs=1,
        per_device_train_batch_size=4,
        gradient_accumulation_steps=8,
        learning_rate=1e-4,      # higher LR for pretraining
        lr_scheduler_type="cosine",
        warmup_ratio=0.05,
        optim="adamw_8bit",
        fp16=True, seed=42,
    ),
)
trainer.train()
# Then fine-tune the pretrained model with SFT (instruction data)
Continued pretraining injects domain knowledge. Follow up with SFT instruction tuning on the same domain for best results.
🎯 Train on Responses Only (SFT — mask prompt loss)
# ⚙️ MIXING TWO LIBRARIES — keep them straight:
#   SFTTrainer            → from TRL (trl 0.24.0)
#   train_on_responses_only → from UNSLOTH (re-exported from unsloth_zoo 2026.4.9)
# It WRAPS an existing trainer and rewrites the prompt's labels to -100.
# On the plain-TRL path you would NOT use this — you'd set
# SFTConfig(completion_only_loss=True) instead (see the Loss-Masking chapter).
from trl import SFTTrainer                              # TRL
from unsloth.chat_templates import train_on_responses_only  # Unsloth

trainer = SFTTrainer(
    model=model, tokenizer=tokenizer,
    train_dataset=dataset,
    dataset_text_field="text",
    max_seq_length=2048, packing=False,
    args=TrainingArguments(...),
)

# Verified signature (unsloth_zoo.dataset_utils.train_on_responses_only):
#   (trainer, instruction_part=None, response_part=None, force_match=True,
#    tokenizer=None, return_function=False, num_proc=None, last_response_only=False)
# Wrap trainer to mask prompt tokens (force_match=True also matches newlines):
trainer = train_on_responses_only(
    trainer,
    instruction_part = "<|im_start|>user\n",        # ChatML (SmolLM2/Qwen)
    response_part    = "<|im_start|>assistant\n",   # must match template byte-for-byte
)
trainer.train()

# For Llama-3 instruct format:
# instruction_part = "<|start_header_id|>user<|end_header_id|>\n\n"
# response_part    = "<|start_header_id|>assistant<|end_header_id|>\n\n"
Response-only training = model only learns from its own outputs, not from memorising user prompts. Often improves quality.
🔤
Tokenizer & Vocabulary Surgery
Extra tokens, embedding resizing, and when to retrain the embeddings

Adding tokens and training embeddings is powerful and easy to get wrong. The honest default is you probably don't need new tokens — and when you do, there's an exact ritual. Reference: Chat Templates & Data Prep cheatsheet. 🧪 Runnable: scripts/18_token_surgery.py verifies every step on SmolLM2-135M — measured: LaTeX→11 tokens, JSON→9 (no surgery needed), and a new token's embedding row lands 0.0001 from the pretrained mean (vs 2.36 average spread).

Start from the default: you almost certainly don't need new tokens. A tokenizer is just a compression scheme, and BPE already compresses anything built from ASCII — a LaTeX equation or a JSON blob becomes a handful of ordinary sub-word tokens the model has seen billions of times. A brand-new token does the opposite of what you'd hope: it starts life as a random vector sitting in a hole, while every other token rests on a carefully learned manifold. Until you train that row, it's noise.

That's the ritual these cards walk: first prove you need tokens at all, then add them seated on the existing manifold rather than at random, and only then decide whether to actually train those embedding rows — knowing it's the single most expensive thing you can bolt onto a LoRA run. The last card is the bill that arrives when you skip the first question.
🧪 First: do you even need them?
# Check what the tokenizer already does with your content
len(tok(r"$\hat{H}\psi=E\psi$").input_ids)   # LaTeX = ASCII → fine
len(tok('{"k": 1}').input_ids)             # JSON = ASCII → fine
LaTeX, JSON, HTML, code and chemical formulae are plain ASCII — BPE handles them. Adding tokens you don't need just dilutes training signal.
➕ Adding tokens correctly
tok.add_special_tokens(
   {"additional_special_tokens": ["<rxn>", "</rxn>"]})
model.resize_token_embeddings(len(tok),
                            mean_resizing=True)   # init at embedding mean
mean_resizing=True puts new rows on the pretrained manifold (far better than random). It's simple but collapses many new tokens into one subspace — fine for a few control tokens, risky for hundreds.
🧠 Retraining the embeddings
# Add embed_tokens + lm_head to LoRA targets ONLY when:
#  • CPT shifts the token distribution a lot, OR
#  • you added new tokens (their rows start near-random)
target_modules += ["embed_tokens", "lm_head"]
embedding_learning_rate = 5e-6   # < base LR, or they destabilize
Connects to the LR chapter: big layers want a small LR. New rows must be trained or they stay meaningless.
⚠️ The cost (when NOT to)
Targeting embed_tokens+lm_head on a 3B blew the adapter from 116 MB → 1.2 GB (measured). On tiny SFT data over existing tokens it also overfits.
For plain SFT on existing tokens: leave embeddings out. Train them for CPT or genuinely new vocabulary — not by default.
6
Unsloth — Fast SFT Fine-Tuning
2× faster · 60% less VRAM · Full end-to-end workflow

Unsloth re-implements the hot kernels (RoPE, attention, cross-entropy) in Triton and patches them into Hugging Face models at load time — no model changes required. The result is ~2× faster training and ~60% less VRAM versus a plain PEFT/QLoRA setup. It's the go-to choice for supervised fine-tuning on a single GPU with limited memory.

① Load + ② Attach LoRA
from unsloth import FastLanguageModel

model, tokenizer = FastLanguageModel.from_pretrained(
    model_name    = "unsloth/Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-bnb-4bit",
    max_seq_length= 2048,
    load_in_4bit  = True,   # False → 16-bit LoRA
    dtype         = None)

model = FastLanguageModel.get_peft_model(model,
    r=16, lora_alpha=16, lora_dropout=0,
    target_modules=["q_proj","k_proj","v_proj","o_proj",
                    "gate_proj","up_proj","down_proj"],
    use_rslora=True,
    use_gradient_checkpointing="unsloth")
③ Dataset + ④ Train
from unsloth.chat_templates import get_chat_template
from datasets import load_dataset
from trl import SFTTrainer
from transformers import TrainingArguments
from unsloth import is_bfloat16_supported

tokenizer = get_chat_template(tokenizer, chat_template="chatml")
ds = load_dataset("mlabonne/FineTome-100k", split="train")
ds = ds.map(lambda x: {"text": [
    tokenizer.apply_chat_template(m, tokenize=False)
    for m in x["conversations"]]}, batched=True)

trainer = SFTTrainer(model=model, tokenizer=tokenizer,
    train_dataset=ds, dataset_text_field="text",
    max_seq_length=2048, packing=True,
    args=TrainingArguments(output_dir="output",
        num_train_epochs=1, per_device_train_batch_size=8,
        gradient_accumulation_steps=2, learning_rate=3e-4,
        lr_scheduler_type="linear", warmup_steps=10,
        optim="adamw_8bit", weight_decay=0.01,
        fp16=not is_bfloat16_supported(),
        bf16=is_bfloat16_supported(), seed=42))
trainer.train()
⑤ Inference
FastLanguageModel.for_inference(model)
from transformers import TextStreamer

inputs = tokenizer.apply_chat_template(
    [{"from":"human","value":"Explain LoRA simply."}],
    tokenize=True, add_generation_prompt=True,
    return_tensors="pt").to("cuda")

model.generate(input_ids=inputs,
    streamer=TextStreamer(tokenizer),
    max_new_tokens=256, temperature=0.7,
    do_sample=True, use_cache=True)
⑥ Save — 3 Ways
# A — adapters only (~100MB)
model.save_pretrained("lora_adapters")
tokenizer.save_pretrained("lora_adapters")

# B — merged 16-bit (~16GB, best for deploy)
model.save_pretrained_merged("merged", tokenizer,
    save_method="merged_16bit")
model.push_to_hub_merged("user/MyModel",
    tokenizer, save_method="merged_16bit")

# C — GGUF for Ollama / llama.cpp
for q in ["q4_k_m","q8_0"]:
    model.push_to_hub_gguf("user/MyModel-GGUF", tokenizer, q)
# ollama run hf.co/user/MyModel-GGUF
⚡ GPU times (100k samples, 1 epoch): A100 40GB ≈ 4h45m  |  L4 ≈ 19h  |  Free T4 ≈ 47h  |  VRAM: QLoRA 8B ≈ 5.5GB  —  see 15_lora_footprint.py to reproduce the param-count & VRAM figures.
Script: 08_unsloth_sft.py — the full ①–⑥ workflow above, runnable.
🎭
Loss Masking, Chat Templates & Learning Rates
The SFT mechanics that decide whether training learns the right thing

Three knobs quietly decide the fate of an SFT run: which tokens carry the loss, which chat template wraps them, and what learning rate moves which weights. Get them wrong and the loss still goes down — you've just taught the wrong thing. Quick reference: SFT Trainer cheatsheet · Chat Templates cheatsheet. 🧪 Runnable: scripts/05_sft.py trains with completion-only loss and prints the unmasked fraction (measured 78–81%).

The one idea behind all three knobs: a language model only ever learns to predict the next token — so "fine-tuning" is really just choosing which next-token predictions you grade it on. Masking is that choice made explicit: every masked token is a prediction you've decided not to grade. Completion-only SFT is you saying "I don't care if you can echo the user's question, only the answer that follows it." Mask the wrong span and you're grading the wrong exam — the loss still falls, it just falls on the wrong thing.

The cards below follow that thread end to end: the loss modes are the menu of grading schemes, the chat template physically marks the prompt/answer boundary the mask keys off, and the learning rate sets how hard each graded mistake shoves the weights. The last card is the ten-second check that proves you wired it up right — before you burn a GPU-hour discovering you didn't.
🧭 Your dataset's shape decides the training type — TRL auto-detects it
This is the single most important table for not repeating your last run's mistake. You don't tell TRL "do CPT" or "do SFT" — it infers the job from the columns in your dataset. Get the columns right and masking follows automatically; get them wrong and you silently train the wrong objective. Field names and behavior below are quoted from the official TRL Dataset formats guide and the SFT Trainer docs.
Your dataset columns TRL calls it Default loss (what's graded) This is effectively…
{"text": "..."} Language modeling (standard) Every token (full sequence) CPT / raw-text fine-tuning
{"messages": [...]} Language modeling (conversational) Every token, unless assistant_only_loss=True chat-formatted CPT, or assistant-only SFT
{"prompt": ..., "completion": ...} Prompt-completion Completion only (prompt auto-masked to -100) standard SFT (Q→A)
{"prompt": [...], "completion": [...]} Prompt-completion (conversational) Completion only; combine with assistant_only_loss=True for multi-turn conversational SFT
{"prompt", "chosen", "rejected"} Preference preference loss (not next-token) DPO / ORPO (not SFTTrainer)
Quoted behavior: "By default, the trainer computes the loss on the completion tokens only, ignoring the prompt tokens" (prompt-completion); and for completion_only_loss=None (the default), "loss is computed on the completion for prompt-completion datasets, and on the full sequence for language modeling datasets." So the rule of thumb: a bare "text" column ⇒ full-sequence (CPT-style) loss; a prompt+completion pair ⇒ completion-only (SFT) loss. If your "SFT" used a "text" column, it was actually doing CPT on the whole string — a very common reason SFT underperforms CPT.
Trainer→format mapping (from the TRL docs): SFTTrainer → language-modeling or prompt-completion · DPOTrainer → preference · GRPOTrainer → prompt-only · RewardTrainer → preference. Full reference: Which dataset type to use?
🤔 Check yourself (Socratic)
Your last SFT came out worse than CPT. Ask: what columns did that dataset have? If it was a single "text" field, which loss did TRL actually compute — and was the model ever graded on producing an answer versus reciting the whole string? How would you have caught it in ten seconds before training?
💡 In plain words (Feynman)
A "text" column tells TRL "this is one document — grade every token," i.e. CPT. To get real SFT you must hand it prompt+completion so it knows where the answer starts and masks the rest. The ten-second check: print (labels != -100).float().mean() — CPT ≈ 1.0, correct SFT ≈ 0.2–0.8. A 1.0 on a "SFT" run means you were doing CPT all along.
✅ The three ways to mask loss in TRL — verified against trl 0.24.0 / transformers 5.5.0
There is no masking=... argument. In modern TRL you control what gets graded by the shape of your dataset plus one flag. Pick the row that matches your data — do not mix them up. Every snippet below was run against the installed stack; the masked fractions are measured, not guessed.
# ════ WAY 1 — prompt/completion dataset → completion_only_loss (THE DEFAULT) ════
# Dataset rows look like: {"prompt": "...", "completion": "..."}
# TRL tokenizes prompt, tokenizes prompt+completion, and sets the prompt's
# labels to -100. You do NOT touch the chat template yourself.
from trl import SFTTrainer, SFTConfig
from datasets import load_dataset

ds = load_dataset("trl-lib/kto-mix-14k", split="train")   # has prompt+completion
trainer = SFTTrainer(
    model="Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct",
    args=SFTConfig(completion_only_loss=True),  # True is the DEFAULT for prompt/completion
    train_dataset=ds,
)
trainer.train()
# completion_only_loss=False would grade the prompt too (that's CPT-style, usually wrong for Q&A)
# ════ WAY 2 — conversational dataset → assistant_only_loss ════
# Dataset rows look like: {"messages": [{"role":"user",...},{"role":"assistant",...}]}
# Loss is computed ONLY on assistant turns (great for multi-turn chat).
args = SFTConfig(assistant_only_loss=True)
# ⚠️ REQUIRES the chat template to contain {% generation %} ... {% endgeneration %} markers.
# TRL auto-patches known families (Qwen3, Llama-3, GPT-OSS). For others it will FAIL.
Measured gotcha (this is the silent one — verified just now on the installed stack): SmolLM2-135M-Instruct's stock template does not contain the {% generation %} block. Calling apply_chat_template(msgs, return_assistant_tokens_mask=True, return_dict=True) does not crash — it prints a warning ("return_assistant_tokens_mask==True but chat template does not contain {% generation %} keyword") and returns an all-zero assistant mask (measured: sum(assistant_masks)=0 of 43). So assistant_only_loss=True on such a model can mask out everything — training on nothing — instead of erroring loudly. On a model without the marker, use WAY 1 (prompt/completion + completion_only_loss), or supply one of TRL's patched training templates. TRL bundles them and swaps them in automatically for these families when assistant_only_loss=True: Cohere, Cohere2, DeepSeek-V3, Gemma/Gemma3, GLM-4-MoE, GPT-OSS, Llama 3/3.1/3.2, Phi-3/3.5, Qwen2.5, Qwen3 (per TRL Chat Templates docs). SmolLM2 is not on that list, so it won't be auto-patched.
# ════ WAY 3 — legacy collator (string-match the response prefix) ════
# Older but explicit: mask everything before a literal response marker.
from trl import DataCollatorForCompletionOnlyLM
from transformers import AutoTokenizer

tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("facebook/opt-350m")
collator = DataCollatorForCompletionOnlyLM(
    response_template="### Assistant:",        # tokens before this → -100
    instruction_template="### Human:",        # (optional) for multi-turn
    tokenizer=tok,
)
# Gotcha: context-sensitive tokenizers (Llama) encode "### Assistant:" differently
# mid-text vs at line start. If masking fails, pass token IDs instead of the string:
ids = tok.encode("\n### Assistant:", add_special_tokens=False)[2:]  # drop the leading \n tokens
💬 How specifying a chat template loads it — the part that wrecked the last run
SFT only works if the template used in training is the one you use at inference. A base model usually has no chat template at all — train it as if it had one and your prompts won't match later, so the model looks broken. Modern TRL fixes this with one argument:
