# Give a base model a chat template by pointing at a model that HAS one.
# SFTTrainer copies that template onto your tokenizer AND aligns special tokens
# (e.g. EOS) automatically — verified present in SFTConfig on trl 0.24.0.
trainer = SFTTrainer(
    model="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B-Base",                  # a BASE model, no template
    args=SFTConfig(
        output_dir="Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct",
        chat_template_path="HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM3-3B",  # borrow this model's template
    ),
    train_dataset=load_dataset("trl-lib/Capybara", split="train"),
)
trainer.train()
If the base model already HAS a template (e.g. Qwen2.5), don't clone one — just make sure the EOS token matches it, or generations won't stop: 
SFTConfig(eos_token="<|im_end|>")   # align EOS with the template, per HF docs
And when you give SFTTrainer a conversational dataset, it applies the chat template for you — do not pre-render it into a "text" field and also let the trainer template it, or you double-wrap the special tokens (a classic cause of "my SFT was worse than CPT"). Render it yourself only if you pass plain {"text": ...} rows.
🔬 Prove the mask landed — measured on SmolLM2-135M-Instruct just now
Reproducing exactly what WAY 1 does to one prompt/completion row, then printing which tokens survive masking:
from transformers import AutoTokenizer
tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M-Instruct")
prompt     = [{"role":"user",      "content":"What is LoRA?"}]
completion = [{"role":"assistant", "content":"A low-rank bypass."}]

p = tok.apply_chat_template(prompt, tokenize=False, add_generation_prompt=True)
f = tok.apply_chat_template(prompt+completion, tokenize=False)
p_ids, f_ids = tok(p).input_ids, tok(f).input_ids
labels = [-100]*len(p_ids) + f_ids[len(p_ids):]   # what completion_only_loss does

learned = [tok.decode([i]) for i,l in zip(f_ids, labels) if l != -100]
# MEASURED OUTPUT:
#   total tokens : 49     masked(-100): 38     learned: 11   (frac learned 0.22)
#   LEARNED TEXT : 'A low-rank bypass.<|im_end|>\n'   ← only the answer + stop token ✓
The 11 learned tokens are exactly the assistant's answer and its <|im_end|>. The 38 prompt tokens are ignored. If your "learned" span included the question, your mask is wrong — fix it before training, not after.
🎯 The 3 loss modes
ModeLoss onFor
full causalevery tokenCPT / raw text
completion-onlyanswer (prompt masked)SFT Q&A
assistant-onlyassistant turnsmulti-turn chat
"Masking" = setting masked labels to -100 so CrossEntropyLoss(ignore_index=-100) skips them. It's the loss that's masked, not attention.
💬 The chat template IS the mask boundary
# completion-only loss finds the boundary from the template:
# <|im_start|>user … <|im_end|>          ← masked (-100)
# <|im_start|>assistant  ANSWER <|im_end|> ← learned
text = tok.apply_chat_template(msgs, tokenize=False,
                                  add_generation_prompt=False)
Forget the mask and the model learns to generate the question too → at inference it "echoes the prompt back." The classic SFT bug.
📈 Learning rates that work
WhatLRWhy
LoRA adapters1e-4 – 3e-4tiny matrices tolerate a high LR
embeddings / lm_head~5e-6huge layers → small steps or they destabilize
full fine-tune1e-5 – 5e-5all weights move
Use warmup_ratio≈0.03 + a cosine schedule. Unsloth supports a separate embedding_learning_rate for the CPT case.
✅ Prove the mask (one line)
lab = next(iter(trainer.get_train_dataloader()))["labels"]
print((lab != -100).float().mean())
# CPT  → 1.00 (nothing masked)
# SFT  → 0.78–0.81 (prompt masked)   ← measured in the case study
If your SFT number is 1.0, the prompt isn't masked. Catch it in seconds, not after a wasted run.
🩺 The label health-check — the one diagnostic that catches the silent killers
Before you spend a GPU-hour, look at one collated example and count how many label positions are actually graded (not -100). This single number diagnoses the two most common "training looked fine but the model is broken" failures.
# Inspect the FIRST real training example the trainer will see
example = trainer.train_dataset[0]
non_masked = sum(1 for l in example["labels"] if l != -100)
total      = len(example["labels"])
print(f"unmasked: {non_masked}/{total} ({100*non_masked/total:.1f}%)")

# Healthy ranges (rule of thumb):
#   completion_only_loss=True  → ~30–70% unmasked   (prompt is -100)
#   assistant_only_loss=True   → ~20–60% unmasked   (user/system are -100)
#   neither (CPT / text)       → ~100% unmasked      (everything graded)
#   ── 0% unmasked → STOP. You will train on nothing (loss looks fine, model learns zip).
What 0% means, by mode: with assistant_only_loss=True, your template is missing the {% generation %} markers (the silent all-zero mask from the chapter above — measured on SmolLM2). With the manual DataCollatorForCompletionOnlyLM path, your response_template string doesn't byte-match what the tokenizer produced. Either way the fix is upstream of training, and this one line catches it in seconds. (This is the same check the 🧪 Case Study runs at Steps 4–5.)
SymptomCause → fix
Loss flatlines / ZeroDivisionError: All labels are -1000% unmasked — template/marker mismatch. Run the check above; fix the columns or template before retraining.
Model echoes the prompt back at inference~100% unmasked on a "SFT" run — you forgot completion-only loss, so it learned to generate the question too. Use prompt+completion + completion_only_loss=True.
Loss drops but generation is gibberishTrained a base model with a chat template but never added the template's special tokens → model sees <|im_start|> as noise. Use SFTConfig(chat_template_path=…), which adds tokens + resizes embeddings for you.
🎤 Inference must mirror training — the mismatch that fakes a "broken model"
A huge share of "my fine-tune doesn't work" reports are not training bugs — they're formatting mismatches at inference. You trained on the chat template; you must generate with the same template, this time asking the model to start the assistant turn:
# Reload base + your adapter (or the merged model)
from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer
tok   = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("out-sft")
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("out-sft").eval().to("cuda")

messages = [{"role": "user", "content": "What is the Hartree-Fock method?"}]
inputs = tok.apply_chat_template(
    messages, return_tensors="pt",
    add_generation_prompt=True,        # ← INFERENCE: append "<|im_start|>assistant\n" so it starts its turn
).to("cuda")

out = model.generate(inputs, max_new_tokens=128, do_sample=True, temperature=0.7)
# Decode ONLY the new tokens — slice off the prompt you fed in:
print(tok.decode(out[0][inputs.shape[1]:], skip_special_tokens=True))
The three lines that bite people: (1) add_generation_prompt=True — training used False (full turn); inference uses True (open turn). Get this backwards and the model has nothing to continue from. (2) out[0][inputs.shape[1]:] — slice off the prompt or you'll "decode" your own question back. (3) a base model has no template — feed raw text and let it continue; only instruct/SFT models take the chat path. On Unsloth, call FastLanguageModel.for_inference(model) first for its faster generation path.
🎛️ Training parameters that actually move the needle — defaults read from installed trl 0.24.0
Every default below was read straight from the docstrings in the installed trl/trainer/sft_config.py — not from memory. SFTConfig subclasses transformers.TrainingArguments, so you get every standard arg plus these; the † marks defaults TRL deliberately overrides from transformers.
Parameter Default Lib What it does (per the installed docstring)
completion_only_lossNoneTRLNone = auto: completion-only for prompt-completion data, full sequence for language-modeling data. True forces completion-only (prompt-completion only).
assistant_only_lossFalseTRLLoss only on assistant turns; conversational datasets only; needs {% generation %} markers in the template.
max_length1024TRLTokenized length cap; longer sequences truncated from the right. None = no truncation. With packing on, it sets the block length. 1024 is shorter than many expect — long samples get silently cut.
packingFalseTRL"Group multiple sequences into fixed-length blocks to improve computational efficiency and reduce padding." See the packing card below.
packing_strategy"bfd"TRL"'bfd' (best-fit decreasing, default), or 'wrapped'." With bfd, padding-free turns on automatically.
padding_freeFalseTRLFlatten the batch into one continuous sequence, no padding. "Only supported with FlashAttention 2 or 3."
eval_packingNoneTRLPack the eval set; if None, uses the same value as packing.
chat_template_pathNoneTRLSet the model's chat template from a tokenizer/Hub id, or a Jinja file (then you must add referenced special tokens and resize embeddings yourself).
eos_tokenNoneTRLEnd-of-turn token; defaults to processing_class.eos_token. Align with your template or generations won't stop.
pad_tokenNoneTRLDefaults to processing_class.pad_token; if that's also None, falls back to the EOS token.
loss_type"nll"TRL"nll" (standard) or "dft" (Dynamic Fine-Tuning).
dataset_num_procNoneTRLProcesses for dataset preprocessing (used when packing=False). Set =1 on the Unsloth path — see the Engines chapter.
learning_rate2e-5 †TRL"The initial learning rate for AdamW." †transformers default is 5e-5. For LoRA adapters, usually raise to ~1e-4.
gradient_checkpointingTrue †TRL"Save memory at the expense of slower backward pass." †transformers default is False.
bf16None→True †TRLbf16 mixed precision; "if not set, defaults to True if fp16 is not set." Needs Ampere+ / XPU / NPU / CPU. †transformers default is False.
logging_steps10 †TRLLog every N steps (a float in [0,1) = ratio of total steps). †transformers default is 500.
The workhorses you'll set on nearly every run live in transformers.TrainingArguments (inherited by SFTConfig), so their defaults come from transformers, not TRL — confirm them against your installed version: num_train_epochs, per_device_train_batch_size, gradient_accumulation_steps (effective batch = batch_size × accumulation × #GPUs), warmup_ratio (a small value like 0.03 stabilizes the start), and lr_scheduler_type (e.g. "cosine"). Print any default live with from trl import SFTConfig; SFTConfig.__dataclass_fields__["max_length"].default.
📦 Packing — what it really does, and the one way it bites you
Most fine-tuning batches are mostly padding: to make a rectangular tensor you pad every short example up to the longest in the batch. On one-line Q&A pairs, the GPU can spend the majority of its FLOPs multiplying zeros. Packing concatenates many examples into dense max_length blocks so almost every token is real work — the installed docstring: "group multiple sequences into fixed-length blocks to improve computational efficiency and reduce padding."
from trl import SFTConfig
args = SFTConfig(
    packing=True,
    packing_strategy="bfd",    # best-fit decreasing (default); also enables padding-free
    max_length=2048,           # with packing on, this is the BLOCK length
)
The bite: the older "wrapped" strategy concatenates all tokens into a stream and cuts fixed-length blocks, so one example can span a boundary and a block can mix two unrelated examples — which can hurt quality. The modern default "bfd" packs whole examples and, combined with padding_free, keeps them from attending across each other — but that correctness depends on FlashAttention 2/3. So: if you pack, prefer "bfd" on a flash-attention build; note dataset_num_proc is ignored while packing is on; and for short-context or small runs, leaving packing=False is perfectly fine — the win shows up at scale.
7
Fine-Tuning Methods — Full Comparison
SFT · Causal LM · DPO · ORPO · GRPO · Pretraining — know which to reach for

Choosing the wrong training method wastes GPU hours. SFT teaches format; Causal LM teaches domain knowledge; DPO / ORPO align the model to human preferences without a live reward model; GRPO uses verifiable rewards (math answers, code execution) for self-improvement. The table below shows exactly when each method applies and what data it needs.

📊 Method Comparison at a Glance
MethodData formatLoss targetWhen to useTRL class
Continued Pretraining Raw text documents All tokens (CLM) Inject domain knowledge into base model SFTTrainer (no template)
Causal LM FT Raw text / completions All tokens (CLM) Style/domain adaptation, base model training Trainer + DataCollatorForLanguageModeling
SFT (instruction) {instruction, response} pairs All or response-only tokens Teach model to follow instructions SFTTrainer
DPO {prompt, chosen, rejected} triples Preference margin loss Align after SFT — prefer chosen over rejected DPOTrainer
ORPO {prompt, chosen, rejected} triples SFT loss + odds ratio penalty SFT + alignment in one pass, no reference model ORPOTrainer
GRPO Prompts + reward function Group relative policy reward Reasoning / math — reward-based RL (DeepSeek-R1 style) GRPOTrainer
🟩 Causal LM Fine-Tuning
# Train on raw text — no chat template needed.
# DataCollatorForLanguageModeling shifts labels automatically.
# Use when: domain adaptation, style transfer, continued pretraining.
from transformers import (AutoTokenizer, AutoModelForCausalLM,
    DataCollatorForLanguageModeling, Trainer, TrainingArguments)
from datasets import load_dataset

tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained("gpt2")
tok.pad_token = tok.eos_token
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("gpt2")
model.config.pad_token_id = tok.eos_token_id

# Your domain text — one document per row in "text" column
ds = load_dataset("text", data_files="corpus.txt", split="train")
ds = ds.map(lambda x: tok(x["text"], truncation=True, max_length=512),
          batched=True, remove_columns=["text"])

collator = DataCollatorForLanguageModeling(tok, mlm=False)
# mlm=False → next-token prediction; labels = input_ids shifted by 1

trainer = Trainer(
    model=model,
    args=TrainingArguments(
        output_dir="./clm-out",
        num_train_epochs=3,
        per_device_train_batch_size=4,
        learning_rate=2e-5,
        fp16=torch.cuda.is_available(),
    ),
    train_dataset=ds,
    data_collator=collator,
    processing_class=tok,
)
trainer.train()
Use Causal LM FT to shift the model's knowledge and style before SFT. Run on domain corpus first, then SFT on instruction pairs.
🔵 DPO — Direct Preference Optimization
# Data: {prompt, chosen, rejected}
# {"prompt":"Q:...", "chosen":"good answer", "rejected":"bad answer"}
from trl import DPOTrainer, DPOConfig
from datasets import load_dataset

model_name = "username/my-sft-model"  # start from SFT model
tok   = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    model_name, torch_dtype="auto", device_map="auto")

ds = load_dataset("mlabonne/orpo-dpo-mix-40k", split="train")
# needs columns: prompt, chosen, rejected

trainer = DPOTrainer(
    model=model,
    ref_model=None,              # None = use initial model as ref
    args=DPOConfig(
        output_dir="./dpo-out",
        num_train_epochs=1,
        per_device_train_batch_size=4,
        learning_rate=5e-7,        # very small LR for DPO
        beta=0.1,                  # KL penalty strength
        fp16=True,
    ),
    processing_class=tok,
    train_dataset=ds,
)
trainer.train()
DPO = most common post-SFT alignment step. Run SFT first, then DPO on preference data.
🟢 ORPO — One-Pass SFT + Alignment
# Same data format as DPO but trains SFT + alignment together.
# No separate reference model needed → faster + cheaper.
# NOTE: ORPO lives in trl.experimental.orpo (not top-level trl)
from trl.experimental.orpo import ORPOTrainer, ORPOConfig

model_name = "meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B"  # start from BASE model
tok   = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    model_name, torch_dtype="auto", device_map="auto")

ds = load_dataset("mlabonne/orpo-dpo-mix-40k", split="train")

trainer = ORPOTrainer(
    model=model,
    args=ORPOConfig(
        output_dir="./orpo-out",
        num_train_epochs=1,
        per_device_train_batch_size=4,
        learning_rate=8e-6,
        beta=0.1,               # odds ratio weight
        max_length=1024,     # max_prompt_length removed in trl 1.x
        fp16=True,
    ),
    processing_class=tok,
    train_dataset=ds,
)
trainer.train()
ORPO skips the SFT step — go straight from base model to aligned model in one training run.
🟠 GRPO — RL from Reward (DeepSeek-R1 style)
# GRPO = model generates multiple responses per prompt,
# scores them with a reward function, updates policy.
# Used for reasoning / math / verifiable tasks.
from trl import GRPOTrainer, GRPOConfig

# Define reward function (returns float per response)
def reward_fn(completions, **kwargs):
    # Example: reward correct math answers
    rewards = []
    for c in completions:
        answer = extract_answer(c)   # your parsing logic
        rewards.append(1.0 if answer == correct_answer else 0.0)
    return rewards

ds = load_dataset("openai/gsm8k", "main", split="train")
# gsm8k has "question" column — rename to "prompt" (required by GRPOTrainer)
ds = ds.map(lambda x: {"prompt": x["question"]}, remove_columns=["question"])

trainer = GRPOTrainer(
    model=model,
    reward_funcs=[reward_fn],   # reward_funcs is a LIST of callables
    args=GRPOConfig(
        output_dir="./grpo-out",
        num_train_epochs=1,
        per_device_train_batch_size=4,
        learning_rate=1e-6,
        num_generations=4,            # must be <= per_device_train_batch_size
        max_completion_length=512,    # GRPOConfig field (verified trl 0.24.0, default 256). NOT max_new_tokens
        fp16=True,
    ),
    processing_class=tok,
    train_dataset=ds,
)
trainer.train()
GRPO needs a verifiable reward signal — best for math, code, structured output. Hard to apply to open-ended chat.
📌 Recommended Training Pipeline
For a general assistant:
  1. Start from a strong base model (Llama, Qwen, Mistral)
  2. Optional: Continued pretraining on your domain corpus
  3. SFT on instruction pairs (Unsloth + SFTTrainer)
  4. DPO on preference data to align outputs
For a reasoning / math model:
  1. Start from instruct model
  2. GRPO with verifiable reward (accuracy, format)
For fastest path to aligned model:
  1. Base model → ORPO (SFT + alignment in one shot)
LR guidelines: Pretraining 1e-4 → SFT 3e-4 → DPO 5e-7 → GRPO 1e-6
Always use a lower LR for alignment than for SFT.
Part IV
Proving It Works
A fine-tune you did not measure is a fine-tune you did not finish
↩ The model is trained. Before you trust it, prove it improved — and check it didn't forget what it already knew.
📊
How to Know If Fine-Tuning Worked
Loss curves · Perplexity · Generation quality · Overfitting detection

Fine-tuning without evaluation is flying blind. You need to check at least three things: is the loss decreasing?, is the model generating better outputs?, and is it overfitting? This section gives you the concrete patterns.

📉 1. Loss Curves
# After trainer.train(), inspect the log history
result = trainer.train()

for entry in trainer.state.log_history:
    if "loss" in entry and "eval_loss" not in entry:
        print(f"step {entry['step']:>4d}  loss={entry['loss']:.4f}")
    if "eval_loss" in entry:
        print(f"epoch {entry['epoch']}  eval_loss={entry['eval_loss']:.4f}")
Train loss should decrease steadily. If eval loss spikes while train loss drops — you're overfitting.
📐 2. Perplexity
import math

# Perplexity = exp(eval_loss)
# Lower = model assigns higher probability to correct tokens
metrics = trainer.evaluate()
ppl = math.exp(metrics["eval_loss"])
print(f"perplexity: {ppl:.2f}")

# Compare before vs after fine-tuning
# baseline_ppl=328 → after_ppl=9 = significant improvement
Perplexity is the standard LM metric. Always compute it before AND after fine-tuning to measure improvement.
🔍 3. Generation Quality Check
# Use the same prompts before and after training
test_prompts = ["LoRA injects", "The learning rate", "GRPO uses"]

for prompt in test_prompts:
    inputs = tok(prompt, return_tensors="pt").to(device)
    with torch.no_grad():
        out = model.generate(**inputs, max_new_tokens=40, temperature=0.7)
    print(tok.decode(out[0], skip_special_tokens=True))

# BEFORE: "LoRA injects a new layer of complexity into the game..."  (generic)
# AFTER:  "LoRA injects low-rank matrices into transformer..."       (domain-relevant)
Automated metrics don't catch everything. Always eyeball generations from a fixed set of test prompts.
⚠️ 4. Overfitting Detection
Symptom: train_loss keeps dropping, but eval_loss starts rising after epoch N.
Cause: Model is memorizing training examples instead of learning patterns.
Fixes:
  • Reduce num_train_epochs (often 1–3 is enough for fine-tuning)
  • Add more training data
  • Increase weight_decay (e.g., 0.01 → 0.1)
  • Use eval_strategy="steps" with small eval_steps to catch the turn early
Always pass an eval_dataset to the trainer. Without it, you have no way to detect overfitting.
⚠️ 5. The metric format-trap — when perplexity LIES
The single most expensive evaluation mistake: judging an instruction model with a raw-text metric. An SFT'd model "wants" chat tokens, so on plain text it looks worse — even when it's far better at the actual task. SFT can raise domain perplexity while completion perplexity collapses, because the two losses pull in opposite directions. 
# MiniCPM5-1B, measured before/after SFT (case study §6):
#   completion ppl  107.68 -> 3.64   (-97%)   ← SFT's real job, huge win
#   domain ppl       24.81 -> 34.90   (+41%)  ← rose! raw-text metric is misleading here
#   keyword recall      5% -> 46%     (9x)    ← format-robust, confirms the win
Rule: match the metric to the phase. Judge CPT by domain perplexity; judge SFT by completion perplexity and a generation-based metric (keyword recall / ROUGE) that doesn't punish format. Never rank a base-vs-instruct decision on one perplexity number. Full metric playbook: Metrics cheatsheet.
See it in action: scripts/10_evaluation.py runs a full before/after comparison — loss tracking, perplexity measurement, generation quality checks — all in one script. The format-trap is measured live in the 🧪 Case Study (case_study/scripts/07_eval.py).
🧠
Catastrophic Forgetting
When teaching the new makes it forget the old — detect it, then prevent it

Fine-tuning can quietly erode general ability while it learns your domain. The good news: with LoRA it's rare, and it's trivial to measure so you never ship it blind. Seen in the 🧪 Case Study (we checked, and didn't forget). 🧪 Runnable: scripts/04_cpt_base_vs_instruct.py measures domain vs general perplexity before/after CPT.

📄 Full runnable code — case_study/scripts/04_cpt_base_vs_instruct.py · 182 lines (click to expand)
# verbatim from case_study/scripts/04_cpt_base_vs_instruct.py @ 4e1e381 — re-sync if the script changes · run with the repo .venv
r"""
04_cpt_base_vs_instruct.py — CPT from a BASE vs an INSTRUCT model, and a forgetting smoke test.

RESEARCH QUESTION #4: when you continue-pretrain on a niche domain, does it matter whether you start
from the base model or the instruct model — and does CPT damage general ability ("catastrophic
forgetting")?

WHAT WE MEASURE (so the answer is shown, not asserted)
------------------------------------------------------
For each starting point (base, instruct) we record, before and after CPT on the chemistry corpus:
  • DOMAIN perplexity on held-out chemistry text  -> should DROP  (the model is learning the domain)
  • GENERAL perplexity on held-out non-chemistry text -> if it RISES, that is forgetting
  • a few GENERAL question generations -> eyeball whether instruction-following degraded
The instruct model has more to lose (it can follow instructions); the base model has little general
"skill" to forget. Mitigations (lower LR, ≤1 epoch, ~10% replay) are in PITFALLS.md / RESEARCH_NOTES.

CONTRACT: TRIAL/FULL flag, self-sufficient (builds domain corpus + fetches a small general corpus if
missing, downloads both models), idempotent (caches outputs/forgetting_<mode>.json), reproducible.

Run:  python case_study/04_cpt_base_vs_instruct.py            # TRIAL (default)
      CASE_STUDY_MODE=full python case_study/04_cpt_base_vs_instruct.py
"""
from __future__ import annotations
import argparse
import importlib.util
import json
import sys
from pathlib import Path

import torch

sys.path.insert(0, str(Path(__file__).resolve().parent))
import config  # noqa: E402
import utils   # noqa: E402


def _sib(fname: str, name: str):
    spec = importlib.util.spec_from_file_location(name, Path(__file__).resolve().parent / fname)
    m = importlib.util.module_from_spec(spec); spec.loader.exec_module(m)
    return m


_CB = _sib("01_build_corpus.py", "cb_for_04")     # fetch_plaintext, clean, slugify
_CPT = _sib("03_cpt.py", "cpt_for_04")            # load_corpus_texts, pack_blocks, perplexity


def general_corpus_texts() -> list[str]:
    """Fetch + cache a small NON-chemistry corpus for the forgetting metric (self-sufficient)."""
    gdir = config.DATA / "general"
    gdir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    texts = []
    for title in config.GENERAL_PAGES:
        f = gdir / f"{_CB.slugify(title)}.txt"
        if not f.exists():
            resolved, raw = _CB.fetch_plaintext(title)
            txt, _ = _CB.clean(raw)
            f.write_text(txt)
        texts.append(f.read_text())
    return texts


def _train_cpt(model, lim):
    """Attach LoRA (incl. embeddings, split LR) and run CPT. Returns the trained PEFT model."""
    from transformers import Trainer, TrainingArguments
    from peft import LoraConfig, get_peft_model
    model = get_peft_model(model, LoraConfig(
        r=config.CPT["lora_r"], lora_alpha=config.CPT["lora_alpha"],
        lora_dropout=config.CPT["lora_dropout"], use_rslora=config.CPT["use_rslora"],
        target_modules=config.CPT["target_modules"], task_type="CAUSAL_LM"))
    emb = [p for n, p in model.named_parameters()
           if p.requires_grad and ("embed_tokens" in n or "lm_head" in n)]
    rest = [p for n, p in model.named_parameters()
            if p.requires_grad and not ("embed_tokens" in n or "lm_head" in n)]
    opt = torch.optim.AdamW([{"params": rest, "lr": config.CPT["lr"]},
                             {"params": emb, "lr": config.CPT["embedding_learning_rate"]}])
    return model, opt


def run_one(model_name: str, tag: str, is_instruct: bool, domain_texts, general_texts, lim) -> dict:
    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer, Trainer, TrainingArguments
    from datasets import Dataset

    device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
    block = config.CPT["max_seq_len"]
    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)
    if tok.pad_token is None:
        tok.pad_token = tok.eos_token

    # held-out blocks (per this model's tokenizer)
    dom_all = _CPT.pack_blocks(tok, domain_texts, block)
    n_held = min(lim["eval_blocks"], max(1, len(dom_all) // 5))
    dom_held, dom_train = dom_all[-n_held:], dom_all[:-n_held]
    gen_held = _CPT.pack_blocks(tok, general_texts, block)[: lim["eval_blocks"]]

    print(f"\n=== {tag} ({model_name}) ===")
    model = utils.load_causal_lm(model_name, training=True)   # 4-bit QLoRA if active model needs it
    dom_before = _CPT.perplexity(model, dom_held, device)
    gen_before = _CPT.perplexity(model, gen_held, device)
    print(f"  before CPT: domain ppl {dom_before:.2f} | general ppl {gen_before:.2f}")
    utils.generate_samples(model, tok, config.GENERAL_QUESTIONS, n=lim["gen_samples"],
                           chat=is_instruct, title=f"{tag} GENERAL answers BEFORE CPT")

    model, opt = _train_cpt(model, lim)
    ds = Dataset.from_dict({"input_ids": dom_train, "labels": [b[:] for b in dom_train],
                            "attention_mask": [[1] * len(b) for b in dom_train]})
    targs = TrainingArguments(
        output_dir=str(config.OUTPUTS / f"cpt_{tag}_{config.RUN_MODE}" / "ckpt"),
        per_device_train_batch_size=config.CPT["batch_size"],
        gradient_accumulation_steps=config.CPT["grad_accum"],
        num_train_epochs=1, max_steps=lim["cpt_max_steps"], learning_rate=config.CPT["lr"],
        gradient_checkpointing=config.GRAD_CKPT,           # essential for a 3B in 4-bit on 12GB
        gradient_checkpointing_kwargs={"use_reentrant": False} if config.GRAD_CKPT else None,
        bf16=(device == "cuda"), logging_steps=5, save_strategy="no", report_to=[], seed=config.SEED)
    Trainer(model=model, args=targs, train_dataset=ds, optimizers=(opt, None)).train()

    dom_after = _CPT.perplexity(model, dom_held, device)
    gen_after = _CPT.perplexity(model, gen_held, device)
    dom_delta = 100 * (dom_after - dom_before) / dom_before
    gen_delta = 100 * (gen_after - gen_before) / gen_before
    print(f"  after CPT:  domain ppl {dom_after:.2f} ({dom_delta:+.1f}%) | "
          f"general ppl {gen_after:.2f} ({gen_delta:+.1f}%  <- forgetting if positive)")
    utils.generate_samples(model, tok, config.GENERAL_QUESTIONS, n=lim["gen_samples"],
                           chat=is_instruct, title=f"{tag} GENERAL answers AFTER CPT")

    del model
    if device == "cuda":
        torch.cuda.empty_cache()
    return dict(tag=tag, model=model_name, is_instruct=is_instruct,
                domain_ppl_before=dom_before, domain_ppl_after=dom_after, domain_delta_pct=dom_delta,
                general_ppl_before=gen_before, general_ppl_after=gen_after, general_delta_pct=gen_delta)


def run(force: bool = False) -> dict:
    config.set_all_seeds()
    lim = config.limits()
    out = config.OUTPUTS / f"forgetting_{config.RUN_MODE}.json"
    if out.exists() and not force:
        m = json.loads(out.read_text())
        print(f"[cached] §4 forgetting ({config.RUN_MODE}) — use --force to rerun.")
        print(json.dumps(m["summary"], indent=2))
        return m

    domain_texts = _CPT.load_corpus_texts()
    general_texts = general_corpus_texts()
    print(f"=== §4 base-vs-instruct CPT + forgetting | mode={config.RUN_MODE} ===")
    base = run_one(config.BASE_MODEL, "base", False, domain_texts, general_texts, lim)
    inst = run_one(config.INSTRUCT_MODEL, "instruct", True, domain_texts, general_texts, lim)

    print("\n=== SUMMARY: domain learning vs general forgetting ===")
    print(f"  {'start':9} | {'domain ppl Δ':>13} | {'general ppl Δ':>14}")
    print(f"  {'-'*9}-+-{'-'*13}-+-{'-'*14}")
    for r in (base, inst):
        print(f"  {r['tag']:9} | {r['domain_delta_pct']:>+12.1f}% | {r['general_delta_pct']:>+13.1f}%")
    print("\n  Reading it: domain Δ negative = learned the domain; general Δ positive = forgetting.")
    print("  Expectation: the INSTRUCT start has more general ability to lose, so watch its general Δ")
    print("  and its AFTER answers above. Mitigate with lower LR, ≤1 epoch, and ~10% replay (PITFALLS.md).")

    m = dict(mode=config.RUN_MODE, base=base, instruct=inst,
             summary={"base_domain_delta_pct": base["domain_delta_pct"],
                      "base_general_delta_pct": base["general_delta_pct"],
                      "instruct_domain_delta_pct": inst["domain_delta_pct"],
                      "instruct_general_delta_pct": inst["general_delta_pct"]},
             env=config.record_env())
    out.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    out.write_text(json.dumps(m, indent=2))
    print(f"\n  metrics saved to {out}. Next: §5 SFT (completion-only loss).")
    return m


def main() -> None:
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="CPT base-vs-instruct + catastrophic-forgetting smoke test.")
    ap.add_argument("--force", action="store_true")
    ap.add_argument("--mode", choices=["trial", "full"])
    args = ap.parse_args()
    if args.mode:
        config.set_mode(args.mode)
    run(force=args.force)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
It sounds dramatic, and for full fine-tuning it can be — push a model hard on a narrow domain and it starts failing arithmetic it used to ace, because every weight is free to drift toward your data and away from everything it knew. The reassuring news: LoRA has forgetting nearly designed out of it. The base weights are frozen, so the original model is still physically present under the adapter — pop the adapter off and you have it back, bit for bit.

That reframes the topic from "how do I prevent a disaster" to "how do I notice a small one" — and noticing is cheap. Hold out a little general text, measure its perplexity before and after, watch whether it rises. The cards are exactly that loop: detect with one number, understand why LoRA gives you a net, and reach for mitigations (fewer epochs, a little replay, a modest rank) only if the number actually moves. The cardinal sin isn't forgetting — it's shipping a model that forgot without ever checking.
🔬 Detect it (cheap)
# perplexity on GENERAL text, before AND after training
gen_before = perplexity(model0, tok, general_text)
gen_after  = perplexity(model1, tok, general_text)
# rising general PPL = forgetting
# measured (SmolLM3-3B): 8.33 → 8.19  → NO forgetting
Always hold out a small general-text set and check it alongside your domain metric. One number tells you if you broke the base.
🛡️ Why LoRA helps
The base weights are frozen; only small adapters move. You can even remove the adapter to recover the original model exactly. Full fine-tuning has no such safety net — every weight drifts.
🧯 Mitigate it
• Fewer epochs / lower LR — most forgetting comes from over-training on a narrow set.
• Replay / data mixing — blend a little general instruction data into your domain set.
• Keep LoRA rank modest — huge adapters on tiny data overfit (and overwrite more).
• Adapter isolation — keep domain skills in separate adapters, swap at inference (see Adapter Patterns) instead of one over-trained model.
🔌
Adapter Patterns — Save, Load, Swap
One base model · Many specialists · Hub integration · Merge for deploy
Why Adapters Matter
A LoRA adapter is ~1% the size of the base model. Save it, share it, swap it — without duplicating the base weights.

Base model (~500 MB) + 10 adapters (~10 MB) = 10 specialized models

This is how production systems work: one base loaded in memory, adapters hot-swapped per request.
Save & Reload
Save — only adapter weights, not the full model:
model.save_pretrained("./my-adapter/")

Creates just two files: adapter_config.json (~1 KB) + adapter_model.safetensors (~MB).

Reload onto a fresh base:
PeftModel.from_pretrained(base, "./my-adapter/")

Gotcha: Use PeftModel.from_pretrained() to load. get_peft_model() is for creating a new adapter.
Merge for Deployment
Bake adapter weights into the base model — no PEFT dependency at inference:
model = model.merge_and_unload()

Result: a plain transformers model. Ship it, export to GGUF, deploy anywhere.

Gotcha: merge_and_unload() is not in-place. You must assign the return value.

Trade-off: merged model = full size again. Can't swap adapters anymore.
HuggingFace Hub
Adapters live on the Hub just like full models. Push ~5 MB instead of ~5 GB:
model.push_to_hub("my-org/hvac-adapter")

Pull from anywhere:
PeftModel.from_pretrained(base, "my-org/hvac-adapter")

Or auto-detect the base model:
AutoPeftModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("my-org/hvac-adapter")

Real example: ybelkada/opt-350m-lora — a 6 MB adapter for OPT-350M.
Multi-Adapter Swap
Load multiple adapters onto one base model and switch per request:

model = PeftModel.from_pretrained(base, path_a, adapter_name="hvac")
model.load_adapter(path_b, adapter_name="energy")
model.set_adapter("hvac")   # → HVAC specialist
model.set_adapter("energy") # → energy specialist

Gotcha: load_adapter() does not activate the adapter. Always call set_adapter() after loading.
Quick Reference
Create adapter get_peft_model(base, config)
Save model.save_pretrained(path)
Load PeftModel.from_pretrained(base, path)
Push to Hub model.push_to_hub(repo_id)
Auto-load AutoPeftModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(repo_id)
Merge model = model.merge_and_unload()
Add adapter model.load_adapter(path, name)
Switch model.set_adapter(name)
The Adapter Registry Pattern
One base model + named adapters = an organizational knowledge registry. Each adapter encodes domain expertise that lives independently — versioned, auditable, swappable.
Add knowledge
Train a new adapter
Retire knowledge
Delete the adapter
Update knowledge
Retrain, push new version
An organization that maintains an adapter registry owns its AI knowledge — versioned like code, auditable like data, independent of any vendor. That's the difference between consuming AI and building AI capability.
Experimental Results — Domain Specialization Proof
Can a 124M parameter model + tiny adapter beat a 355M model on domain-specific text? We trained two LoRA adapters (HVAC and Energy) on GPT-2 (124M) with just 120 sentences each and 100 training steps, then compared against GPT-2 Medium (355M) — a model 3× larger. Perplexity = how surprised the model is by the text (lower = better).
Model Params HVAC Text ↓ Energy Text ↓ Generic Text ↓
GPT-2 (base) 124M 352 140 89
GPT-2 Medium 355M 340 135 44
GPT-2 + HVAC adapter 124M + 1MB 296 ✓ — —
GPT-2 + Energy adapter 124M + 1MB — 110 ✓ —
The Result
124M + 1 MB adapter beats 355M on domain text.
HVAC: 296 vs 340 (13% better).
Energy: 110 vs 135 (19% better).
The Tradeoff
The bigger model wins on generic text (44 vs 89).
Adapters create specialists, not generalists.
That's the point — use each for what it's good at.

Results from scripts/14_adapter_save_load.py — run it yourself to reproduce. Training: 120 sentences, 100 steps, batch 16, lr 3e-4. Validated: zero data leakage between train/test, 93% vocabulary distinctness between domains, identical tokenizer for fair comparison.
